Cohomogeneity one actions on symmetric spaces of mixed type
Tomás Otero, Ivan Solonenko, Hiroshi Tamaru

Abstract
. In this article, we study isometric cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces of mixed type, i.e., those whose universal cover splits as a nontrivial product of symmetric spaces of compact, noncompact, and Euclidean types. We provide a new family of “diagonal” cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces of the form ℝ n × M − \mathbb{R}^{n}\times M_{-} , where M − M_{-} is of noncompact type. We show that, with the exception of this family, any cohomogeneity-one action on a symmetric space decomposes as a product of isometric actions on its compact, Euclidean, and noncompact factors. This fully reduces the classification problem for cohomogeneity-one actions to symmetric spaces of a single type.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
53C35, 57S20

1.
Introduction and main results

A smooth isometric action of a Lie group on a Riemannian manifold is of cohomogeneity one if its regular orbits are hypersurfaces. Cohomogeneity-one actions have long been a topic of great interest in Riemannian geometry. For instance, they can be used to construct Riemannian metrics with various special properties, such as Einstein metrics
[
Böh98
]
, metrics with special holonomy
[
BS89
,
FH17
]
, or metrics of positive curvature
[
GWZ08
]
: the usual procedure consists of modifying the given metric on a space
M
along the orbits of a cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
(e.g., by means of rescaling or a Cheeger deformation) to produce a new
\mathsf{H}
-invariant metric with the desired properties. Cohomogeneity-one actions are also of significance from the viewpoint of submanifold geometry: their orbits form an isoparametric family, and the principal orbits are homogeneous hypersurfaces and thus have constant principal curvatures.

A natural question to ask in this regard is whether one can list all possible cohomogeneity-one actions on a given Riemannian manifold. By design, this kind of investigation makes particular sense for homogeneous spaces, since the existence of such actions calls for a large isometry group. To make this into a classification problem, one needs a suitable notion of equivalence. One such notion—natural from the standpoint of submanifold geometry—is that of
orbit equivalence
: two actions are
orbit-equivalent
if the corresponding orbit foliations of the ambient space are isometrically congruent.

Historically, the first spaces to receive a classification of cohomogeneity-one actions were the Euclidean and real hyperbolic spaces. In these manifolds, every isoparametric family is given by the orbits of a cohomogeneity-one action, so the classification follows from the classical works of Segre
[
Seg38
]
and Cartan
[
Car38
]
on isoparametric hypersurfaces. The same approach does not work for other spaces: for example, round spheres admit isoparametric hypersurfaces that are not homogeneous
[
FKM81
]
. A classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on the spheres was achieved with different methods by Hsiang and Lawson in
[
HL71
]
.

Obtaining a complete classification for general symmetric spaces is still an ongoing effort, with many contributions over the last twenty five years.
For instance, a classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on irreducible symmetric spaces of compact type was achieved by Kollross
[
Kol02
]
.
For reducible symmetric spaces of compact type, the problem is still open (see
[
Kol17
]
). In the noncompact setting, this problem turned out to be more involved. In fact, it was not until very recently that a complete classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces of noncompact type was achieved by the second author and Sanmartín-López in
[
SS25
]
. This result was a culmination of a long list of contributions by several authors
[
BB01
,
BDT10
,
BD15
,
BT03
,
BT04
,
BT07
,
BT13
,
DDO23a
,
DDR21
,
Sol23
,
Sol21
]
(see
[
DDO23b
]
for a recent survey on the topic). It should be noticed that, unlike in the compact case, the classification for symmetric spaces of noncompact type was accomplished even in the case when the ambient space is reducible (see
[
DDO23a
]
).

So far, cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces have been studied on a type-by-type basis. Recall that the universal Riemannian covering space of a symmetric space splits as a Riemannian product
M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
, where
M_{0}
is a Euclidean space and
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
are symmetric spaces of compact and noncompact types, respectively. In this article, we consider symmetric spaces for which the above decomposition is nontrivial, which we call
of mixed type
. We study cohomogeneity-one actions on such spaces via the behavior of the action on each individual factor.

Our main result asserts that with the only exception of a certain family of “diagonal” actions (which we parameterize explicitly), any other cohomogeneity-one action on a symmetric space of mixed type
M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
decomposes: that is, it has the
same orbits as some product action
\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{-}\curvearrowright M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
(see Section
3
for a more rigorous treatment of decomposability of actions in the general context of Riemannian products). This fully reduces the problem of classifying cohomogeneity-one actions on any symmetric space to that on its compact, Euclidean, and noncompact factors.

Theorem A
.

Let
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be a simply connected symmetric space, where
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
are symmetric spaces of compact and noncompact type, respectively.
Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly and isometrically with cohomogeneity one.
Then, either the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes, or it is orbit-equivalent to the action of
I(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\subseteq I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
is a Lie subgroup inducing a homogeneous codimension-one foliation of diagonal type on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
as described below.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that the theorem is formulated only for simply connected symmetric spaces. In Section
6
, we explain how to dispense with this restriction and give a version of Theorem
A
for non-simply connected symmetric spaces (see Theorem
6.4
).

Remark 1.2
.

By construction—which we lay out below—the action
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
can only be defined when both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are nontrivial. If either of them is trivial, any cohomogeneity-one action on
M
decomposes.

Let us introduce some context and notation and define the action of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
appearing in Theorem
A
(more details about the structure of symmetric spaces can be found in Section
2
). Any Riemannian symmetric space can be written as a quotient
M=\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}
, where
\mathsf{G}=I^{0}(M)
and
\mathsf{K}
is the isotropy subgroup of
\mathsf{G}
at some fixed base point
o\in M
. The isometry Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
admits a natural reductive decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
with
\mathfrak{k}=\mathrm{Lie}(K)
and
\mathfrak{p}\cong T_{o}M
. If
M
is simply connected, it splits into a Riemannian product
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
as described above, and we have the corresponding splittings
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathsf{G}_{0}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
,
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{K}_{+}\times\mathsf{K}_{0}\times\mathsf{K}_{-}
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
, etc. Here
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is a noncompact real semisimple Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{k}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{-}
is a Cartan decomposition. We fix a restricted root space decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\bigoplus\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
and consider the corresponding Iwasawa decomposition
\mathsf{G}_{-}=\mathsf{K}_{-}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{N}
. The solvable subgroup
\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
acts on
M_{-}
simply transitively and provides the so-called
solvable model
of
M_{-}
. Note that the action of the translation group
\mathbb{R}^{n}\trianglelefteq I^{0}(M_{0})
on
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
is also simply transitive. Let
\mathfrak{r}^{n},\mathfrak{a},
and
\mathfrak{n}
stand for the Lie algebras of
\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathsf{A},
and
\mathsf{N}
, respectively, and pick any nonzero vectors
E\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and
X\in\mathfrak{a}
. By using the properties of the root space decomposition, it can be easily checked that the subspace

\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)\oplus\mathbb{R}(E+X)\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\ominus X)\oplus\mathfrak{n},

is a codimension-one subalgebra of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
, where by
\mathfrak{v}\ominus Y
we mean the orthogonal complement of a vector
Y
in a subspace
\mathfrak{v}
of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\cong T_{o}(M_{0}\times M_{-})
with respect to the inner product induced by the metric on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. The connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
of
I(M_{0}\times M_{-})
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and without singular orbits. As we will show in Section
4
, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
form a family of mutually isometrically congruent hypersurfaces, all of them of constant mean curvature
\frac{||E||}{||X||\sqrt{||E||^{2}+||X||^{2}}}\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\alpha(X)
. The orbit foliation of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
will be called a
homogeneous codimension-one foliation of diagonal type
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. Figure
1
shows what such a foliation looks like in the case of
M_{0}=\mathbb{R}
and
M_{-}=\mathbb{R}H^{2}
.

Figure 1.
A homogeneous foliation of diagonal type on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}H^{2}
.
Here
\mathbb{R}H^{2}
is represented by the Poincaré disk model in the horizontal plane.

The road towards Theorem
A
consists of three main steps. First, we study whether a given isometric action on a Riemannian product decomposes based on its behavior on the factors. As an application, we will obtain the following:

Theorem B
.

Let
M
be a simply connected compact Riemannian manifold,
N
a Hadamard manifold, and
\mathsf{H}
a connected Lie group acting on
M\times N
properly and isometrically. If the action has cohomogeneity one, then it decomposes.

Consider now a simply connected symmetric space
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
. Since
M_{+}
is compact and
M_{0}\times M_{-}
is Hadamard, Theorem
B
implies that we can reduce the study of cohomogeneity-one actions on
M
to that on
M_{+}
and
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. This motivates us to study cohomogeneity-one actions on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
and investigate which of them decompose. For our second step, we work under the assumption that such an action has no singular orbits and thus induces a homogeneous Riemannian foliation. We are able to show that if the action does not decompose, it must be orbit-equivalent to the action of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
for some choices of
E
and
X
as defined above. This allows us to give a complete classification of homogeneous codimension-one foliations on any (simply connected) symmetric space:

Theorem C
.

Let
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be a simply connected symmetric space, where
M_{+}=\mathsf{G}_{+}/\mathsf{K}_{+}
is of compact type,
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
M_{-}=\mathsf{G}_{-}/\mathsf{K}_{-}
is of noncompact type.
Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly, isometrically, with cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits.
Then, the action of
\mathsf{H}
is orbit-equivalent to one of the following:

(a)

The action of
\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
.

(b)

The action of
\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{X}
,
where
\mathsf{H}_{X}
induces a codimension-one foliation of horospherical type on
M_{-}
.

(c)

The action of
\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{i}
,
where
\mathsf{H}_{i}
induces a codimension-one foliation of solvable type on
M_{-}

(d)

The action of
\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
,
where
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
induces a codimension-one foliation of diagonal type on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
.

Remark 1.3
.

If any of the factors in the decomposition
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
are trivial, it may not be possible to define some of the actions appearing above, and one should read Theorem
C
by removing such items from the list.
For instance, if
M_{0}
is nontrivial and
M_{-}=\{*\}
, then the action of
\mathsf{H}
is orbit-equivalent to the action described in (a).
If
M_{0}=\{*\}
and
M_{-}
is nontrivial, then the action of
\mathsf{H}
is orbit-equivalent to one of the actions described in (b) and (c).
Finally, if both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are trivial, none of the actions in (a)–(d) can be defined and
M=M_{+}
admits no homogeneous codimension-one foliations.

Note that the action in (a) comes from the action of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
on
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
by translations, whose orbits are parallel hyperplanes. This is the the only homogeneous codimension-one foliation on the Euclidean space up to congruence. Homogeneous codimension-one foliations on symmetric spaces of noncompact type have been studied extensively in
[
BDT10
,
BT03
,
Sol21
]
and correspond to two families of codimension-one subgroups of the solvable model of
M_{-}
. More precisely, the actions giving rise to so-called foliations of
horospherical type
in (b) are given by connected Lie subgroups
\mathsf{H}_{X}\subseteq\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
with Lie algebras of the form

\mathfrak{h}_{X}=(\mathfrak{a}\ominus X)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}

for some nonzero
X\in\mathfrak{a}
. The orbits of such an action are mutually congruent hypersurfaces. For certain choices of
X
, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{X}
are horospheres (see
[
DST21
, Rem. 5.4]
). The actions producing foliations of
solvable type
in (c) are characterized by the fact that they have exactly one minimal orbit. They are given by connected Lie subgroups
\mathsf{H}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
with Lie algebras

\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{n}\ominus X)

for some nonzero vector
X
in a simple root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
. Finally, the actions in (d) correspond to the new family of examples
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
described above.

Remark 1.4
.

The actions of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathsf{G}_{-},\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{X}
, and
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
can be viewed as part of a broader family of homogeneous foliations on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
:
namely, if
\mathfrak{u}
is any subspace of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
, then
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\left((\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a})\ominus\mathfrak{u}\right)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
.
The action of the corresponding connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
gives rise to a (regular) homogeneous hyperpolar foliation with
\mathsf{U}\cdot o
as a section (
hyperpolar
meaning that the action is polar and its sections are flat), where
\mathsf{U}
is the connected Lie subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{A}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}
.
Moreover, since
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
are all mutually congruent (see
[
KT13
]
).
The action of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
decomposes if and only if
\mathfrak{u}=(\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus(\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{a})
. In this case, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathfrak{u}}
are of the form
W\times F
, where
W
is an affine subspace of
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
parallel to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\ominus(\mathsf{U}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
F
is one of the leaves of the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\Phi,V}
described in
[
BDT10
]
with
\Phi=\emptyset
and
V=\mathfrak{a}\ominus(\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{a})
.

For our third and final step, we show that a cohomogeneity-one action on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
that has a singular orbit must necessarily decompose. This, together with Theorems
B
and
C
, completes the proof of Theorem
A
.

Theorem D
.

Let
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
M_{-}=\mathsf{G}_{-}/\mathsf{K}_{-}
be a symmetric space of noncompact type.
Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
properly and isometrically with cohomogeneity one.
If
\mathsf{H}
has a singular orbit, then its action decomposes.

Theorem
A
can be interpreted as a classification result for homogeneous hypersurfaces, as these are precisely the regular orbits of isometric cohomogeneity-one actions. In fact, one can use it to derive explicit classifications of homogeneous hypersurfaces in a vast number of symmetric spaces. The only obstacle on the way to a complete classification for general symmetric spaces arises from the yet mysterious behavior of cohomogeneity-one actions on products of symmetric spaces of compact type (see
[
Kol17
]
). Homogeneous hypersurfaces provide the simplest example of hypersurfaces with constant principal curvatures.
Such hypersurfaces have recently been studied in
[
CS19
,
MSSV25
]
for the ambient spaces
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}H^{n}
(which are symmetric spaces of mixed type). In turn, hypersurfaces with constant principal curvatures belong to the much larger class of hypersurfaces of constant mean curvature, whose study in products of space forms has become a lively area of research after the seminal work of Abresch and Rosenberg
[
AR04
]
. Homogeneous hypersurfaces have another remarkable geometric property: they are
isoparametric
(that is, their locally defined nearby parallel hypersurfaces have constant mean curvature). The study and classification of isoparametric hypersurfaces is also a topic of great interest in Riemannian geometry. In recent years, there has been a particular surge of interest towards such hypersurfaces in products of space forms (see
[
DM21
,
LP24
,
LP25
,
SS23
,
TXY25
]
). It should be noted that the only examples obtained in
[
CS19
,
DM21
,
LP24
,
LP25
,
MSSV25
,
SS23
,
TXY25
]
that do not split as products of the form
P_{M}\times N
or
M\times P_{N}
for some hypersurfaces
P_{M}\subseteq M
and
P_{N}\subseteq N
are precisely the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}H^{m}
for some
E
and
X
.

Example 1.5

((
Homogeneous hypersurfaces in products of space forms
))
.

Consider a connected properly embedded homogeneous hypersurface
P\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
, where
n,k\neq 1
. A straightforward application of Theorem
A
yields that either
P
splits as a product
P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
,
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
, or
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
(where
P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}
,
P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
,
P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
are homogeneous hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}H^{k}
, respectively), or
P
is congruent to
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times P_{\Delta}
, where
P_{\Delta}
is a leaf of a homogeneous codimension-one foliation of diagonal type on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
.

As a special case, if
P
is a homogeneous hypersurface in
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
(resp.,
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
), Theorem
A
implies that
P=P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
P=\mathbb{S}^{n}\times P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
(resp.,
P=P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
or
P=\mathbb{S}^{n}\times P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
), with
P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}
,
P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
, and
P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
as above.
Similarly, a homogeneous hypersurface
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
either is a product
P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\times\mathbb{R}H^{k}
or
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
, or is congruent to a leaf
P_{\Delta}
of a homogeneous codimension-one foliation of diagonal type. It follows from
[
HL71
]
that
P_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}
is congruent to a principal orbit of the action obtained by restricting the isotropy representation of a symmetric space of rank two to the unit sphere. By
[
Seg38
]
,
P_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
is a hyperplane, a sphere, or a (generalized) cylinder. Lastly, we know from
[
Car38
]
that
P_{\mathbb{R}H^{k}}
is a horosphere, a totally geodesic
\mathbb{R}H^{k-1}
or an equidistant hypersurface to it, a tube around a totally geodesic
\mathbb{R}H^{l}
, or a geodesic sphere.

Having dealt with Theorem
A
, we turn our attention to cohomogeneity-one actions on non-simply connected symmetric spaces. For an isometric action of a connected Lie group on a Riemannian manifold
M
, we introduce a simple way to
lift
the action to the universal Riemannian cover
\widetilde{M}
of
M
. Conversely, we investigate when an action on
\widetilde{M}
can
descend
to one on
M
. We show that these two procedures are mutually inverse and respect the cohomogeneity. We also look at how they interact with the orbits and orbit equivalence and inspect when they preserve the properness of an action. This machinery allows us to reduce the study and classification of proper isometric actions on
M
to those on
\widetilde{M}
, provided that we understand the group
\Aut(\pi)
of deck transformations of
\pi\colon\widetilde{M}\to M
and how it embeds into
I(\widetilde{M})
. In the special case when
M
is a symmetric space, this group is well understood and looks particularly simple. This allows us to prove Theorem
6.4
, which can be regarded as an analog of Theorem
A
for non-simply connected symmetric spaces.

One of the main technical difficulties we encounter throughout the article is having to deal with the properness assumption on an action. For example, investigating when the descent of a proper action is proper comprises a big part of Section
6
. There are also several moments when it becomes unavoidable to consider actions that may fail to be proper. One such example is Lemma
3.3
, another is our proof of Theorem
D
in Subsection
5.3
. In fact, our approach to Theorem
D
requires us to consider arbitrary (not necessarily proper) transitive isometric actions on Euclidean spaces. Combining the ideas from
[
Ale75
,
AW76
,
GW88
]
with Alekseevskii’s description of flat metric Lie algebras
[
Ale75
, Prop. 3.1]
, we obtain a general classification of transitive groups of isometries of Euclidean spaces in Subsection
5.1
.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we give a brief exposition of some aspects of the theories of symmetric spaces, real semisimple Lie algebras, and isometric actions. In Section
3
, we develop some structural theory concerning isometric actions on Riemannian products. In particular, we prove some decomposability results that lead to Theorem
B
. In Section
4
we study cohomogeneity-one actions on products of symmetric spaces under the assumption that they do not have singular orbits, where we conclude by proving Theorem
C
and computing some geometric properties of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
. In Section
5
we prove Theorem
D
. Theorem
A
then follows as an immediate consequence of Theorems
B
,
C
, and
D
. Finally, in Section
6
, we explain how to relate isometric actions on a connected Riemannian manifold to those on its universal cover, which lets us generalize Theorem
A
to non-simply connected symmetric spaces.

.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce some basic concepts, terminology, and notation related to isometric actions, Riemannian symmetric spaces, and real semisimple Lie algebras, which will be used throughout the article. We also mention some aspects of the theory of parabolic subgroups of real semisimple Lie groups. For a more detailed exposition, we refer to
[
Hel78
]
for the theory of symmetric spaces,
[
Kna02
]
and
[
OV94
]
for semisimple Lie algebras, and
[
AB15
,
Mic08
]
for isometric actions (see also
[
BCO16
]
).

2.1.
Isometric actions and their orbits

If
\mathsf{H}
is a Lie group acting smoothly on a smooth manifold
M
, then each orbit
\mathsf{H}\cdot p\cong\mathsf{H}/\mathsf{H}_{p}
is a injectively immersed submanifold of
M
. The
cohomogeneity
of an action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
is the lowest codimension of its orbits. Orbits of the lowest codimension (as well as their points) are called
regular
and the other ones are said to be
singular
. The action is said to be
proper
if the map
\mathsf{H}\times M\to M\times M
given by
(h,p)\mapsto(h\cdot p,p)
is proper. Proper actions enjoy a variety of favorable properties: for instance, their orbits are properly embedded, their isotropy subgroups are compact, and the orbit space
M/\mathsf{H}
is Hausdorff. If
M
is a (complete and connected) Riemannian manifold, an action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
is called
isometric
if it preserves the metric of
M
. In this case (after possibly quotienting
\mathsf{H}
by its ineffectiveness kernel) we can assume that
\mathsf{H}
is a Lie subgroup of the isometry group
I(M)
. Then, the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
is proper if and only if
\mathsf{H}
is a closed subgroup of
I(M)
. If all orbits of
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I(M)
are closed, then
\mathsf{H}
has the same orbits as its closure
\overline{\mathsf{H}}
in
I(M)
. For these reasons, it is rather common to restrict oneself to closed subgroups of
I(M)
when studying isometric actions.

Throughout the article, we will occasionally have to work with actions that may fail to be proper. We will make use of the following two facts. First, given a smooth action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
of a connected Lie group
\mathsf{H}
, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}
are not merely immersed but in fact weakly embedded, also known as initial submanifolds (see
[
Mic08
, Th. 6.4]
): any smooth map
f\colon N\to M
that takes values in an orbit
\mathsf{H}\cdot p
is actually smooth as a map to
\mathsf{H}\cdot p
. Second, any isometric action of cohomogeneity zero on a connected Riemannian manifold must be transitive. Indeed, such an action must have an open orbit. Being a Riemannian homogeneous space, that orbit is complete and thus must coincide with the ambient manifold.

If
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
is a proper isometric action with both
\mathsf{H}
and
M
connected, the orbits are equidistant and hence one can define distance in the orbit space
M/\mathsf{H}
, turning it into a metric space. If the action has cohomogeneity one and
M
is complete, the orbit space is a 1-dimensional manifold with boundary and thus has to be homeomorphic to one of the following: the real line
\mathbb{R}
, the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the half-open interval
[0,1)
, or the closed interval
[0,1]
. In this case, either the orbits of
\mathsf{H}
form a regular codimension-one foliation, or else there exist at most two singular orbits, and the regular orbits are tubes around any of the singular ones. There are various relations between the geometry and topology of
M
and
M/\mathsf{H}
. For example, if
M
is compact, then so is the orbit space, and hence the latter is homeomorphic to either
\mathbb{S}^{1}
or
[0,1]
. If
M
is simply connected, the orbit space cannot be
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and the boundary points of
M/\mathsf{H}
correspond precisely to the singular orbits.
If
M
is a Hadamard manifold, then
M/\mathsf{H}
is either
\mathbb{R}
or
[0,1)
. See
[
AB15
, Sect. 6.3]
and
[
BB01
, Sect. 2]
for further details.

2.2.
Symmetric spaces and their type.

Let
M=\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}
be a symmetric space, where
\mathsf{G}=I^{0}(M)
is the identity component of the isometry group of
M
and
\mathsf{K}
is the isotropy group of some fixed base point
o\in M
. As usual, we will use gothic letters to denote the corresponding Lie algebras. If
s_{o}\in I(M)
is the geodesic symmetry at
o
, then
\theta=\Ad(s_{o})
is an involutive automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
. The
(+1)
-eigenspace of
\theta
coincides with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
of
\mathsf{K}
, whereas the
(-1)
-eigenspace
\mathfrak{p}
is naturally identified with the tangent space
T_{o}M
, and we have a reductive decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be the Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}
. A symmetric space is said to be of
compact
,
Euclidean
, or
noncompact
type if the restriction of
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
is negative definite, zero, or positive definite, respectively. If
M
is of compact type, then
M
is compact and has nonnegative curvature; if
M
is of noncompact type, then
M
is a Hadamard manifold, i.e., it is of nonpositive curvature and diffeomorphic to a Euclidean space; lastly, if
M
is of Euclidean type, then it is flat and thus its universal cover is a Euclidean space.

Assume that
M
is simply connected and let
M=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
be its de Rham decomposition, where
M_{0}
is the Euclidean factor. We can write
M_{i}=\mathsf{G}_{i}/\mathsf{K}_{i}
for each
i=0,\ldots,k
just like we did for
M
, which leads to the splittings
\mathsf{G}=\prod_{i}\mathsf{G}_{i}
,
\mathsf{K}=\prod_{i}\mathsf{K}_{i}
, and so on (cf. Lemma
3.4
). Moreover, it follows from Schur’s lemma that for
i\neq 0
,
M_{i}
must be of either compact or noncompact type. By grouping together the
M_{i}
’s of the same type, we get a Riemannian product decomposition
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
, where
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
are symmetric spaces of compact and noncompact types, respectively, and
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Throughout Sections
4
and
5
, it will come in very handy to fix an
\Ad_{\mathsf{K}}
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
that makes
\mathfrak{k}
and
\mathfrak{p}
orthogonal. We do it as follows: first of all, proclaim
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{j}
to be orthogonal for
i\neq j
. Just as we did before, fix an isometry
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The (normal abelian) subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{0}
consisting of translations can be naturally identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
itself. We then have
\mathsf{G}_{0}\cong\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n}
, where
\mathfrak{r}^{n}=\mathfrak{p}_{0}
is an abelian ideal. We write
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{M_{0}}
for the scalar product on
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
induced via
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\cong T_{o}\mathbb{R}^{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
X,Y\in\mathfrak{so}_{n}
and
v,w\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}
, the formula
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X+v}\hskip 0.5pt,{Y+w}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=-\tr(XY)+\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{w}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt_{M_{0}}
defines an
\Ad_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Next, take any
1\leq i\leq k
. The Killing form
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
is negative definite on
\mathfrak{k}_{i}
, and we also have
\mathfrak{k}_{i}\perp\mathfrak{p}_{i}
. If
M_{i}
is of compact type, then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
is also negative definite on
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, and we take
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
to be
-\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
. If
M_{i}
is of noncompact type, then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}
is positive definite on
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, and we take
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{X}\hskip 0.5pt,{Y}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=-\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{i}}(X,\theta Y)
. This inner product satisfies
\langle\ad(X)Y,Z\rangle=-\langle Y,\ad(\theta X),Z\rangle
for any
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{g}_{i}
and, in particular, is
\ad_{\mathfrak{k}_{i}}
-invariant. Putting these together, we get an
\Ad_{\mathsf{K}}
-invariant inner product
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
on the whole
\mathfrak{g}
.

In order to simplify some of the computations, unless mentioned otherwise, we will assume throughout the rest of the article that the metric on
M
comes from the restriction of
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
to
\mathfrak{p}
. Although this might seem restrictive a priori, this assumptions turns out harmless when classifying isometric actions on
M
. Indeed, it is a consequence of Schur’s lemma that any
\mathsf{G}
-invariant Riemannian metric
g
on
M
can be written at the base point
o
as

g_{o}=\lambda_{0}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{p}_{0}\times\mathfrak{p}_{0}}}+\lambda_{1}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}\times\mathfrak{p}_{1}}}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{p}_{k}\times\mathfrak{p}_{k}}}

for some constants
\lambda_{i}>0
. Consequently, for any two
\mathsf{G}
-invariant metrics
g
and
g^{\prime}
on
M
, we have
I^{0}(M,g)=I^{0}(M,g^{\prime})
(see Lemma
3.4
). This implies that an action of a connected Lie group on
M
is isometric with respect to
g
if and only if it is such with respect to
g^{\prime}
(see also
[
DDO23a
, Rem. 2.5]
).

Remark 2.1
.

Note that the subalgebras
\mathfrak{h}_{X}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
in Theorems
A
and
C
have been previously described in terms of the induced inner product on
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
