\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
under the identification
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\cong T_{o}(M_{0}\times M_{-})
. If we define these subalgebras in terms of our fixed inner product
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{-}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
, the result might be different.

2.3.
Structure of symmetric spaces of noncompact type

If
M_{-}=\mathsf{G}_{-}/\mathsf{K}_{-}
is a symmetric space of noncompact type, then
\mathsf{G}_{-}
is a noncompact real semisimple Lie group and
\mathsf{K}_{-}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
. In this case,
\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{k}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{-}
is a Cartan decomposition and
\theta
is the corresponding Cartan involution. Pick a maximal abelian subspace
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{p}_{-}
. For each
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
, define
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}_{-}\mid[H,X]=\lambda(H)X\;\text{for all}\;H\in\mathfrak{a}\}
. If
\lambda\neq 0
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\neq\{0\}
,
\lambda
is called a (restricted) root of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
with respect to
\mathfrak{a}
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
is the root space corresponding to
\lambda
. The set of roots of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
with respect to
\mathfrak{a}
will be denoted by
\Sigma
. The subspace
1
1

1

In the presence of a Euclidean factor
M_{0}
, one has to be careful not to confuse this subspace with the isometry Lie algebra of
M_{0}
.

\mathfrak{g}_{0}
coincides with
Z_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{a})\oplus\mathfrak{a}
, where
Z_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{a})
is the centralizer of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{k}
. The direct sum decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\right)

is called the restricted root space decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
with respect to
\mathfrak{a}
. Moreover, if
\lambda,\,\mu\in\Sigma\cup\{0\}
, we have
\theta\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{g}_{-\lambda}
and
[\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{g}_{\mu}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda+\mu}
. The rank
r
of
M_{-}
coincides with
\dim(\mathfrak{a})
. The restriction of the Killing form
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}
to
\mathfrak{a}
is positive definite, so it gives rise to an isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{a}^{*}
and thus induces an inner product on
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
. The set
\Sigma
constitutes a (possibly nonreduced) root system in
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
. Choose a system
\Sigma^{+}
of positive roots in
\Sigma
and let
\Lambda=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\}\subseteq\Sigma^{+}
be the corresponding set of simple roots. The sum
\mathfrak{n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
is a nilpotent subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
. What is more,
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
splits into a vector space direct sum
\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{k}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
, known as an Iwasawa decomposition. If we write
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{N}
for the connected Lie subgroups of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{n}
, respectively, then we get a global Iwasawa decomposition
\mathsf{G}_{-}=\mathsf{K}_{-}\mathsf{A}\mathsf{N}
(meaning, the multiplication map yields a diffeomorphism
\mathsf{K}_{-}\times\mathsf{A}\times\mathsf{N}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathsf{G}_{-}
). The subgroups
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{N}
are closed and simply connected. Moreover,
\mathsf{A}
normalizes
\mathsf{N}
, so the product
\mathsf{A}\mathsf{N}=\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
is a solvable Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
. The global Iwasawa decomposition implies that
\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
acts on
M_{-}
simply transitively. The pullback of the metric of
M_{-}
under the diffeomorphism
\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}M_{-}
is left-invariant, which allows us to think of
M_{-}
as a simply connected solvable Lie group endowed with a left-invariant metric. This is called the solvable model of
M_{-}
.

2.4.
Parabolic subalgebras of real semisimple Lie algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
be a noncompact real semisimple Lie algebra. A subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is called
parabolic
if it contains a subalgebra conjugate to
Z_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{a})\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
. Geometrically speaking, if
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is the isometry Lie algebra of a symmetric space of noncompact type
M_{-}
, then proper parabolic subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
are the isotropy Lie algebras of points at the ideal boundary
M_{-}(\infty)
under the action of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
.

The conjugacy classes of parabolic subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
can be parameterized by the subsets of the set of simple roots: given
\Phi\subseteq\Lambda
, we can consider the root subsystem of
\Sigma
generated by
\Phi
, namely
\Sigma_{\Phi}=\Sigma\cap\operatorname{span}\{\Phi\}
. We choose
\Sigma_{\Phi}^{+}=\Sigma^{+}\cap\Sigma_{\Phi}
as a system of positive roots for
\Sigma_{\Phi}
and define the following subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
:

\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\Bigg(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{\Phi}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\Bigg),\quad\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}=\hskip 6.00006pt\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}\setminus\Sigma_{\Phi}^{+}}\;\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda},

the first of which is
\theta
-invariant and thus reductive, whereas the second one is nilpotent. It follows from the properties of root spaces that
\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}
normalizes
\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
and therefore

\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}

(1)

is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
containing
Z_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{a})\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
. It is called the parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
associated with the subset
\Phi
, and decomposition (
1
) is known as its Chevalley decomposition. Any parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is conjugate to
\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}
for some choice of
\Phi\subseteq\Lambda
.

We define
\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Phi}\ker(\alpha)
and
\mathfrak{a}^{\Phi}=\mathfrak{a}\ominus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}
. The abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}
normalizes
\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
and its centralizer in
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is
\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}
. The complement
\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}\ominus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}
is also a
\theta
-invariant subalgebra, and we have a Lie algebra direct sum decomposition
\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}
. The induced decomposition

\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}=(\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}

is called the Langlands decomposition of
\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}
.

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
admits a natural grading. Namely, consider
H^{\Phi}=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\Lambda\setminus\Phi}H^{i}
, where
\{H^{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
is the basis for
\mathfrak{a}
dual to
\Lambda
, and write
m_{\Phi}=\delta(H^{\Phi})
, where
\delta
is the highest root in
\Sigma^{+}
. Note that
\lambda(H^{\Phi})\in\{0,1,\ldots,m_{\Phi}\}
for all
\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}
, and
\lambda(H^{\Phi})=0
if and only if
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\Phi}^{+}
. Thus, we have a splitting

\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}=\bigoplus_{\nu=1}^{m_{\Phi}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}^{\nu},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}^{\nu}=\hskip 3.99994pt\bigoplus_{\lambda(H^{\Phi})=\nu}\;\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}.

Given
\lambda\in\Sigma
, we define
\mathfrak{k}_{\lambda}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda})=\mathfrak{k}_{-}\cap(\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\lambda})
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{p}_{-}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda})={\mathfrak{p}_{-}\cap(\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\lambda})}
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{p}_{-}}
are the orthogonal projections to
\mathfrak{k}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{-}
, respectively. Then,

\mathfrak{k}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}\cap\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}\cap\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}_{0}\oplus\biggl(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}_{\Phi}}\mathfrak{k}_{\lambda}\biggr)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}=\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}\cap\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{a}^{\Phi}\oplus\biggl(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}_{\Phi}}\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}\biggr)

are a maximal compactly embedded subalgebra of
\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}
and a Lie triple system in
\mathfrak{p}_{-}
, respectively. The latter means that
\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
corresponds to the tangent space at
o_{M_{-}}
of a connected complete totally geodesic submanifold
B_{\Phi}
of
M_{-}
, called a
boundary component
. The submanifold
B_{\Phi}
is itself a symmetric space of noncompact type whose rank is equal to the cardinality of
\Phi
. We write
2
2

2

The definition of
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
given here is different from the one used in
[
BDT10
,
BT13
]
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
was defined to be the derived subalgebra of
\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}
—which is in general larger and thus fails to be the isometry Lie algebra of
B_{\Phi}
(see
[
Sol23
, Rem. 2.4]
).

\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
for the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
generated by
\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
. This subalgebra is
\theta
-stable and semisimple and it has a natural Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}=[\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi},\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}]\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
. Note that
\mathfrak{a}^{\Phi}
is a maximal abelian subspace of
\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
. The corresponding root system of
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
is given by
\Sigma_{\Phi}
and it has
\Phi
as a system of simple roots.

Write
\mathsf{A}_{\Phi}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}
, and
\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
for the connected (closed) Lie subgroups of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
, respectively. The subgroup
\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}=Z_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi})
normalizes
\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}
and has
\mathfrak{l}_{\Phi}
as its Lie algebra, and the semidirect product
\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}
coincides with the normalizer
N_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi})
. We call
\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}
the parabolic subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
associated with
\Phi
. We further define
\mathsf{K}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}\cap\mathsf{K}_{-}
and
\mathsf{M}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{K}_{\Phi}\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
. These are closed subgroups of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{k}_{\Phi}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\Phi}
, respectively. Note that
\mathsf{M}_{\Phi}
and
\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}
are (possibly disconnected) reductive Lie groups and
\mathsf{K}_{\Phi}
is a maximal compact subgroup in either of them (as well as in
\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}
). The group
\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}
splits as a direct product
\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{M}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi}
. The decompositions

\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{L}_{\Phi}\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}=(\mathsf{M}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi})\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}

are called the Chevalley and Langlands decompositions of
\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}
, respectively.

The orbit of
\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
(which coincides with that of
\mathsf{M}_{\Phi})
through
o
in
M_{-}
is precisely the boundary component
B_{\Phi}
. Moreover,
(\mathsf{G}_{\Phi},\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}\cap\mathsf{K}_{-})=(\mathsf{G}_{\Phi},\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}\cap\mathsf{K}_{\Phi})
is an almost effective Riemannian symmetric pair representing
B_{\Phi}
, meaning, the subgroup of elements of
\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
that act on
B_{\Phi}
trivially is finite. In particular, we have a finite covering
\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}\rightarrow I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
is the isometry Lie algebra of
B_{\Phi}
. The Langlands decomposition of
\mathsf{Q}_{\Phi}
induces a diffeomorphism

B_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}M_{-},

known as the horospherical decomposition of
M_{-}
. This allows us to identify
T_{o_{M_{-}}}(M_{-})\cong\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Throughout the paper (and especially in Subsection
5.2
), we will be particularly interested in maximal proper parabolic subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
. Note that if
\Psi\subseteq\Phi\subseteq\Lambda
, then
\mathfrak{q}_{\Psi}\subseteq\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}
.
Thus, maximal proper parabolic subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
correspond to subsets of simple roots of the form
\Lambda\setminus\{\alpha_{j}\}
for some simple root
\alpha_{j}\in\Lambda
.
For the sake of brevity, we will often drop the index
\Phi
and substitute it by
j
when dealing with maximal proper parabolic subalgebras (e.g., the parabolic subalgebra associated with
\Phi=\Lambda\setminus\{\alpha_{j}\}
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
, and so on).

3.
Isometric actions on products

Given an isometric action on a Riemannian product
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{n}
, it makes sense to ask how much information about the action can be extracted from its behavior on each of the individual factors
M_{i}
. In this section, we introduce a notion of decomposability for isometric actions following the approach used by Kollross for products of symmetric spaces of compact type
[
Kol17
]
. It should be noted that, when trying to carry out this kind of study for general Riemannian manifolds, one encounters some technical difficulties. Namely, a proper isometric action on a Riemannian product may not induce actions on its factors and, even if it does, these induced actions may fail to be proper. We deal with these obstacles and prove some decomposability results for cohomogeneity-one actions.

Given a collection of Lie groups
\mathsf{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{H}_{k}
acting isometrically on (connected) Riemannian manifolds
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}
, one can define the
product action
of
\mathsf{H}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}_{k}
on
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
by the formula

(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\cdot(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})=(h_{1}\cdot p_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\cdot p_{k}),

for
h_{i}\in\mathsf{H}_{i}
and
p_{i}\in M_{i}
. Note that the orbits of this action are precisely the products of the orbits of the actions
\mathsf{H}_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
. We will say that an isometric action of a Lie group
\mathsf{H}
on a product of Riemannian manifolds
M_{1}\times\ldots\times M_{k}

decomposes
if there exist isometric Lie group actions
\mathsf{H}_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i},\,i=1,\ldots,k
, such that the action of
\mathsf{H}
has the same orbits as the product action of
\mathsf{H}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}_{k}
on
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
. (If
k=1
, we say that every action decomposes.) It should be noted that even when the action of
\mathsf{H}
is proper, the actions
\mathsf{H}_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
do not have to be. However, in that case, since the orbits of
\mathsf{H}
are properly embedded, the same is true for the orbits of each of the actions
\mathsf{H_{i}}\curvearrowright M_{i}
. As we mentioned in Subsection
2.1
, for each
i
, the closure
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{i}
in
I(M_{i})
must have then same orbits as
\mathsf{H}_{i}
. Consequently, we can simply replace each
\mathsf{H}_{i}
with
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{i}
, whose action on
M_{i}
is now proper.

Remark 3.1
.

This notion of decomposability naturally depends on the expression of
M
as a Riemannian product. In order to avoid this, in
[
Kol17
]
an isometric action of a Lie group
\mathsf{H}
on a Riemannian manifold
M
was defined to be
decomposable
if there exists a decomposition of
M
as a nontrivial Riemannian product
M=M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
such that the action of
\mathsf{H}
is orbit-equivalent to an action on
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
that decomposes in the above sense.
Although our notion is slightly more restrictive, it is tailored to the study of actions in relation to a fixed decomposition of
M
, such as the decomposition
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
of a simply connected symmetric space.

Let
M=M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
be a product of connected Riemannian manifolds and let
\mathsf{H}
be a Lie subgroup of
I(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I(M_{k})\subseteq I(M)
. Then, the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
naturally induces an isometric action on each of the factors by taking

(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\cdot p_{i}=h_{i}\cdot p_{i}

for
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathsf{H}
and
p_{i}\in M_{i}
. This is known as the
projection action
of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{i}
. The orbits of this action are precisely the projections of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
in
M_{i}
. If we write
\operatorname{pr}_{I(M_{i})}\colon I(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I(M_{k})\rightarrow I(M_{i})
for the projection onto
I(M_{i})
, then the effectivization of the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{i}
is the action of
\operatorname{pr}_{I(M_{i})}(\mathsf{H})
on
M_{i}
, so both actions have the same orbits. If the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes as
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{H}_{i}\curvearrowright\prod_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}
, the action
\mathsf{H}_{i}\curvearrowright M_{i}
must have the same orbits as the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{i}
(or, equivalently, as
\operatorname{pr}_{I(M_{i})}(\mathsf{H})
). Consequently, the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes if and only if it has the same orbits as the product of its projection actions on the factors
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}
.

Remark 3.2
.

In general, the isometry group of a Riemannian product
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
might be larger than
I(M_{1})\times\dots\times I(M_{n})
.
For instance, for any positive integers
p
and
q
, one has
I(\mathbb{R}^{p})\times I(\mathbb{R}^{q})=(\mathsf{O}_{p}\times\mathsf{O}_{q})\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\subsetneq\mathsf{O}_{p+q}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{p+q}=I(\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Thus, it is not always possible to define the projection actions of an arbitrary Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I(M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k})
.

If
M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
is a connected Riemannian product and
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I(M_{k})
is a Lie subgroup, the orbit of
\mathsf{H}
through a point
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
is contained in the product of the orbits of the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on each
M_{i}
through
p_{i}
:

\mathsf{H}\cdot(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\subseteq\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{k}
. Thus, if we denote the cohomogeneity by
\operatorname{cohom}(\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M)
, one has

\operatorname{cohom}(\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{1}\times\dots\times M_{k})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{cohom}(\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{i}).

(2)

As we observed above, if the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes, then it has the same orbits as the product of its projection actions. In that case, equality holds in (
2
). One could ask if the converse implication is also true: does equality in (
2
) imply that the action decomposes? In an attempt to prove this, one might run into the following complication: even when the action of
\mathsf{H}
is proper, its projection actions do not have to be. Fortunately, this does not prove to be an obstacle—at least when the action has cohomogeneity one:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
M=M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
be a complete connected Riemannian product and
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I(M_{1})\times\dots\times I(M_{k})
a closed connected subgroup acting on
M
with cohomogeneity one. Then, the action decomposes if and only if
\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\operatorname{cohom}(\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{i})}=1
.

Proof.

We only need to prove that equality in (
2
) implies that the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes. The equality means that there is precisely one projection action that is of cohomogeneity one and the rest are transitive. Therefore, the product
\mathsf{H}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
of the projection actions is also of cohomogeneity one. Let
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
be a regular point of
M
with respect to the action of
\mathsf{H}
. It must then also be regular with respect to the product of the projection actions. In other words, both orbits
\mathsf{H}\cdot p
and
\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{k}
are hypersurfaces. Since the former orbit is contained in the latter and the latter is weakly embedded, the inclusion
\mathsf{H}\cdot p\hookrightarrow\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}\cdot p_{k}
is smooth. By dimension reasons, this inclusion is open. This means that
\mathsf{H}
acts on the latter orbit isometrically and with cohomogeneity zero, and hence transitively. We have shown that every regular orbit of
\mathsf{H}
is also an orbit of
\mathsf{H}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}
. Since the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
is proper and has cohomogeneity one, it can have at most two singular orbits. Each of those is contained in an orbit of
\mathsf{H}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}
and the latter is connected. This implies that every singular orbit of
\mathsf{H}
is an orbit of
\mathsf{H}\times\cdots\times\mathsf{H}
as well. Overall, the two actions share the same orbits and thus the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes.
∎

Next, we address the question of existence of projection actions. Let
M
be a complete simply connected Riemannian manifold, and let
M=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
be its de Rham decomposition. The following result—formulated in a slightly more general setting—asserts that the isometry group of
M
behaves well with respect to the factors:

Lemma 3.4

(
[
Sol24
, Prop. 2.1.60]
)
.

Let
M=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
be a Riemannian product, where
M_{0}
is connected and flat and
M_{i}
’s are (non-flat) connected irreducible Riemannian manifolds for
1\leq i\leq k
. Then, the group
I(M)
decomposes as a semidirect product

I(M)=[I(M_{0})\times I(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I(M_{k})]\rtimes S_{M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}},

where
S_{M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}}
is the subgroup of the symmetric group
S_{k}
consisting of elements permuting the mutually isometric factors
M_{i},\,1\leq i\leq k
.

When applied to a complete simply connected Riemannian manifold, Lemma
3.4
essentially reads as the uniqueness property of the de Rham decomposition. It follows immediately that, for
M=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
as above, we have
I^{0}(M)=I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I^{0}(M_{k})
. Thus, if
\mathsf{H}
is a connected Lie group acting on
M
by isometries, we can always assume
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I^{0}(M_{k})
without loss of generality, and thus the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on the factors are well-defined. Here is another application of Lemma
3.4
repackaged in a slightly different way:

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
M
and
N
be simply connected complete Riemannian manifolds and suppose that
N
has no Euclidean de Rham factor.
If
\mathsf{H}
is a connected Lie subgroup of
I(M\times N)
, then
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I^{0}(M)\times I^{0}(N)
. In particular, the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
and
N
are well-defined.

Proof.

Let
M=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}
and
N=N_{1}\times\cdots\times N_{l}
be the de Rham decompositions of
M
and
N
, respectively. Then, the de Rham decomposition of
M\times N
can be written as

M\times N=M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k}\times N_{1}\times\cdots\times N_{l}.

We then have

I^{0}(M\times N)=\prod_{i=0}^{k}I^{0}(M_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{l}I^{0}(N_{j})=I^{0}(M)\times I^{0}(N).

In particular, any connected Lie subgroup of
I(M\times N)
is contained in
I^{0}(M)\times I^{0}(N)
.
∎

An iterative application of Lemma
3.5
implies
I^{0}(M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k})=I^{0}(M_{1})\times\cdots\times I^{0}(M_{k})
whenever
M_{i}
’s are simply connected and complete and at most one of them has a nontrivial Euclidean de Rham factor, so the projection actions are well-defined for any connected Lie subgroup of
I(M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{k})
in that case as well.

We now turn our attention to Theorem
B
and consider the special situation when
M
is a compact simply connected Riemannian manifold and
N
is a Hadamard manifold.
In this case,
M
does not have a Euclidean factor, so any isometric action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M\times N
by a connected Lie group has well-defined projection actions.
The next result guarantees that only transitive actions on
M\times N
can project to transitive actions on both
M
and
N
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
M\times N
be a simply connected Riemannian product, where
M
is compact and
N
Hadamard, and let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M\times N
by isometries. If both projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
and
N
are transitive, then so is the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M\times N
.

Proof.

Although
\mathsf{H}
does not have to be compact, a result of Montgomery
[
Mon50
, Th. A]
ensures that there exists a compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}
of
\mathsf{H}
whose action on
M
is still transitive. By Cartan’s fixed point theorem, the projection action of
\mathsf{K}
on
N
must have a fixed point, say
o_{N}
. Pick any point
o_{M}\in M
and consider an arbitrary point
(p,q)\in M\times N
. Since the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
N
is transitive, there exists an element
h\in\mathsf{H}
such that
h\cdot q=o_{N}
. Let
k\in\mathsf{K}
be such that
k\cdot(h\cdot p)=o_{M}
. We have

(kh)\cdot(p,q)=(k\cdot(h\cdot p),k\cdot o_{N})=(o_{M},o_{N}).

Thus,
\mathsf{H}
acts on
M\times N
transitively.
∎

By combining the results of this section, we can now prove Theorem
B
:

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Recall that we are given a product
M\times N
, where
M
is simply connected and compact and
N
is Hadamard. Suppose that
\mathsf{H}
is a connected Lie group acting on
M\times N
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. According to Lemma
3.5
, we may assume that
\mathsf{H}
is a connected closed subgroup of
I^{0}(M)\times I^{0}(N)
. Now, according to Lemma
3.6
, only one of the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
and
N
can be transitive, and the other one has to be of cohomogeneity one. Lemma
3.3
then implies that the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes, which completes the proof.
∎

As discussed in the introduction, this result brings us one step closer to Theorem
A
. Indeed, let
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be a simply connected symmetric space decomposed into three types as usual. Let
\mathsf{H}
be a closed connected subgroup of
I(M)
acting on
M
with cohomogeneity one. Since
M_{+}
is compact and
N=M_{0}\times M_{-}
is Hadamard, Theorem
B
ensures that the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
decomposes with respect to the splitting
M=M_{+}\times N
. Suppose now that
\mathsf{L}
is a connected Lie group acting on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. Then, by Lemma
3.5
, its projection actions on
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are well-defined. If any of these projection actions is of cohomogeneity one, then the action of
\mathsf{L}
decomposes by virtue of Lemma
3.3
. Otherwise,
\mathsf{L}
has to act transitively on both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
, which leads us to:

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be a simply connected symmetric space and let
\mathsf{H}
be a closed connected subgroup of
I(M)
acting on
M
with cohomogeneity one. Then, either the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes, or it is orbit-equivalent to the action of
I(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\Delta}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{\Delta}\subseteq I(M_{0}\times M_{-})
is a closed connected subgroup that acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and whose projection actions on
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are transitive.

In what follows, we will pay special attention to cohomogeneity-one actions on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
that do not decompose and thus induce transitive actions in each of the two factors.

4.
Codimension-one homogeneous foliations on symmetric spaces

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
C
, which describes all possible cohomogeneity-one actions on symmetric spaces without singular orbits.
We begin with two lemmas that will prove useful later on. The first of them follows directly from our discussion in Subsection
2.1
about the orbit spaces of cohomogeneity one actions, and it guarantees the non-existence of codimension-one homogeneous foliations on symmetric spaces of compact type:

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one on a simply connected compact Riemannian manifold
M
. Then, the action of
\mathsf{H}
has two singular orbits.

The next lemma will be of help when dealing with actions on
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
without singular orbits. We formulate it in a more general context of actions preserving a fixed totally geodesic submanifold.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
M=\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}
be a symmetric space, and let
S\subseteq M
be a connected properly embedded totally geodesic submanifold passing through the base point
o
. Let
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{G}
be a Lie subgroup preserving
S
. Then, the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{h})
preserves
T_{o}S\subseteq T_{o}M
.

Proof.

Let
s_{o}
be the geodesic symmetry of
M
at
o
, and let
\Theta\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathsf{G})
be the conjugation by
s_{o}
. Recall that
\mathsf{K}\subseteq\mathsf{G}^{\Theta}
is an open subgroup. The subgroup
\mathsf{F}
of
\mathsf{G}
consisting of all elements preserving
S
is a closed subgroup, and it is invariant under
\Theta
because
S
is invariant under
s_{o}
. Therefore, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}
splits as a vector space direct sum
\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{k})\oplus(\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{p})
, where
\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{k}
is the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{F}\cap\mathsf{K}
and thus can be described as the set of elements in
\mathfrak{k}
preserving
T_{o}S
under the isotropy representation. Since
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{F}
, we have
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{k}
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

From the classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on Euclidean spaces, we know that a closed connected subgroup
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
acting on
M_{0}
with cohomogeneity one and without singular orbits must have parallel hyperplanes as its orbits. Therefore, we can apply Lemma
4.2
to deduce that
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h})
preserves
T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
and thus
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}(\mathsf{H})
preserves
\mathsf{H}\cdot o
.

4.1.
The proof of Theorem
C

We now proceed to give a classification of codimension-one homogeneous foliations of symmetric spaces.
For convenience, we split the proof into a series of smaller results that we prove independently.

Let
M=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be a simply connected symmetric space, where
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
are of compact and noncompact type, respectively, and let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly, isometrically, with cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits. As mentioned in Section
3
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{H}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
. Moreover, Theorem
B
(or more precisely, Corollary
3.7
) guarantees that either the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes or it is orbit-equivalent to the action of
I(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\Delta}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{\Delta}
acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and its projection actions on
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are both transitive. Note that, in case the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes, the fact that it has no singular orbits is equivalent to saying that its non-transitive projection action has no singular orbits.

First, suppose that the action of
\mathsf{H}
decomposes. Lemma
4.1
guarantees that
M_{+}
does not admit cohomogeneity-one actions without singular orbits. Furthermore, the only codimension-one homogeneous foliations of
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
are those given by parallel affine hyperplanes. This corresponds to item (a) in Theorem
C
. Next, assume that the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{-}
is non-transitive and thus induces a homogeneous codimension-one foliation. Such foliations have been extensively studied in
[
BT03
]
,
[
BDT10
]
and
[
Sol21
]
. Every cohomogeneity-one action on
M_{-}
without singular orbits is orbit-equivalent to either the action of the connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{X}
of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebra

\mathfrak{h}_{X}=(\mathfrak{a}\ominus X)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n},

for some unit vector
X\in\mathfrak{a}
, or else to the action of the connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebra

\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{n}\ominus X)

for a unit vector
X
in a simple root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
. These cases correspond to items (b) and (c) in Theorem
C
, respectively.

From now on, we take
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})=(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
to be a closed connected subgroup whose action on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
has cohomogeneity one and no singular orbits and does not decompose. In particular, both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are nontrivial. Thanks to Lemma
3.3
, the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
must be transitive.
As our first step, we show that we can replace
\mathsf{H}
with a possibly smaller subgroup that satisfies a number of extra properties.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists a closed connected solvable subgroup
\mathsf{L}
of
\mathsf{H}
acting on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
freely such that the orbits of
\mathsf{L}
and
\mathsf{H}
coincide. For any such
\mathsf{L}
, we have
\mathfrak{l}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{-})=\{0\}
. Moreover, there exists a choice of
\mathfrak{a}\subsetneq\mathfrak{p}
and
\Sigma^{+}\subsetneq\Sigma
such that
\mathfrak{l}\subseteq(\mathfrak{t}_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}))
, where
\mathfrak{t}_{0}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{l})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{-}\subseteq Z_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}(\mathfrak{a})
are abelian subalgebras.

Proof.

Since
M_{0}\times M_{-}
is a Hadamard manifold, there exists a closed connected solvable subgroup
\mathsf{L}
of
\mathsf{H}
that acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
freely and has the same orbits as
\mathsf{H}
(see
[
Lor25
, Th. 3.9]
). The second statement in the lemma follows immediately from the fact that the action of
\mathsf{L}
is free. Let
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}
denote the projections of
(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
onto
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
, respectively. These are both Lie algebra homomorphisms because
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{r}^{n}
are ideals. Consequently,
\mathfrak{t}_{0}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{l})
is a solvable subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
. It is also a compact Lie algebra because so is
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
. This implies that
\mathfrak{t}_{0}
must be abelian. Similarly,
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(\mathfrak{l})
is a solvable subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, hence it is contained in some maximal solvable subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}\subsetneq\mathfrak{g}_{-}
, also known as a Borel subalgebra. Since the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{-}
is transitive, so is the projection action of
\mathsf{L}
on
M_{-}
, which implies that the connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{b}
also acts on
M_{-}
transitively. It follows from the theory of Borel subalgebras of real semisimple Lie algebras (see, e.g.,
[
Mos61
]
) that there must exist a choice of
\mathfrak{a}\subsetneq\mathfrak{p}
and a system of positive roots
\Sigma^{+}\subsetneq\Sigma
such that
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{t}_{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
, where
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
is a maximal abelian subalgebra of
Z_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}({\mathfrak{a}})
. This concludes the proof.
∎

To simplify the notation, we assume from now on that
\mathsf{H}
itself is solvable and acts on

freely. We fix

and
\Sigma^{+}\subsetneq\Sigma
so that

\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3

\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3

\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3

\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3

\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}))
for some abelian

and

. Consider the projection

(\mathfrak{t}_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3

\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}))\to\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})

along
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}
. It follows from Lemma
4.4
that the restriction of this projection to
\mathfrak{h}
, which we denote by
\pi
, yields a linear isomorphism between
\mathfrak{h}
and
\pi(\mathfrak{h})\cong T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
.

As mentioned above, the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are both transitive, so we may decompose the tangent space to
\mathsf{H}\cdot o
at
o
as

\pi(\mathfrak{h})=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)\oplus\mathbb{R}(E+X)\oplus((\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\ominus X)

for some nonzero vectors
E\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and
X\in\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}
. We now define three subspaces of
\mathfrak{h}
by pulling the summands in the above decomposition back to
\mathfrak{h}
. We denote them by
\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)
,
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}=\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}(E+X))
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{-}=\pi^{-1}((\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\ominus X)
. Given
V\in\pi(\mathfrak{h})
, we denote by
T_{V}
the unique vector in
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}
such that
V+T_{V}\in\mathfrak{h}
. In other words, the map
V\mapsto V+T_{V}
is precisely the inverse of
\pi\colon\mathfrak{h}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\pi(\mathfrak{h})
.

Lemma 4.5
.

\mathfrak{h}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{-}
are subalgebras of
\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

Let
V+T_{V}
and
W+T_{W}
be arbitrary vectors in
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, where
V,W\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
. We compute

[V+T_{V},W+T_{W}]=[V,T_{W}]+[T_{V},W]\in\mathfrak{h},

which lies in
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
because
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}
normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
. It is straightforward to see that
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\cap\mathfrak{h}
is contained in
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
. This shows that
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}
. Similarly, if
Y+T_{Y}
,
Z+T_{Y}
are arbitrary vectors in
\mathfrak{h}_{-}
, one has

[Y+T_{Y},Z+T_{Z}]=[Y,T_{Z}]+[T_{Y},Z]+[Y,Z]\in\mathfrak{h}.

(3)

The first two summands in (
3
) are contained in
\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}
because
\mathfrak{t}_{-}\subseteq Z_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}(\mathfrak{a})
and the latter normalizes
\mathfrak{n}
, whereas the last summand lies in
\mathfrak{n}
. Overall, we see that
[Y+T_{Y},Z+T_{Z}]\in(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\cap\mathfrak{h}
. Similarly to the above, we have
(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\cap\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{-}
, which implies that
\mathfrak{h}_{-}
is also a subalgebra. Lastly,
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}
is one-dimensional, so it is an abelian subalgebra.
∎

The next lemma puts a strong restriction on what
\mathfrak{h}
can look like.

Lemma 4.6
.

In the above notation, we have
X\in\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathfrak{s}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(\mathfrak{h}_{-})
. This is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
whose projection to
\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
along
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
is a linear isomorphism onto
(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\ominus X
. According to
[
BT03
, Prop. 5.4]
, there are two options for
X
: either
X\in\mathfrak{a}
or
X\in\mathbb{R}H_{\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for some simple root
\alpha\in\Lambda
. We consider the latter case first and assume that
X\not\in\mathfrak{a}
, i.e.,
X=aH_{\alpha}+X_{\alpha}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
and nonzero
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
.

We first prove that we may assume without loss of generality that
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
or, in other words,
a=0
. As was shown in
[
BDT10
, Prop. 5.11]
, there exists
g\in\mathsf{N}
such that
\Ad(g)((\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n})\ominus X)=(\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n})\ominus X_{\alpha}
. We consider the conjugate subgroup
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=g\mathsf{H}g^{-1}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{h})
. Clearly,
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
is also a closed connected solvable subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
that acts freely on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. Moreover, the actions of
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
are orbit-equivalent via
\operatorname{Id}_{M_{0}}\times g
. It follows that both projection actions of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
are transitive and the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
does not decompose. Next, notice that
\Ad_{g}
acts on
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n}
trivially and preserves the subalgebras
\mathfrak{n},\,\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n},
and
\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
, even though it may not preserve
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
. In particular, we see that
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
is also contained in
(\mathfrak{t}_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{-}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}))
. The projection of
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
along
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}
, which we denote by
\pi^{\prime}
, is an isomorphism onto
\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
, and the latter can be written as

\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)\oplus\mathbb{R}(E+X^{\prime})\oplus((\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\ominus X^{\prime})

for some nonzero
X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}
. Observe that
\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{h}_{-})
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
, and we have
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{h}_{-}))=\Ad_{g}(\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(\mathfrak{h}_{-}))=\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{s})
. Thanks to
[
BDT10
, Prop. 5.11(i)]
, the projection of
\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{s})
in
\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
along
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
, which coincides precisely with
\pi^{\prime}(\Ad_{g}(\mathfrak{h}_{-}))=(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})\ominus X^{\prime}
, is contained in
\Ad(g)((\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n})\ominus X)=(\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n})\ominus X_{\alpha}
. For dimension reasons, it must coincide with the whole
(\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{n})\ominus X_{\alpha}
, which means that
X^{\prime}
is proportional to
X_{\alpha}
.

By replacing
\mathfrak{h}
with
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
, we may simply write
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. This means that
\mathfrak{a}\subsetneq\pi(\mathfrak{h})
and in particular
H_{\alpha}\in\pi(\mathfrak{h})
. We calculate:

[H_{\alpha}+T_{H_{\alpha}},E+X+T_{E+X}]=\alpha(H_{\alpha})X+[T_{H_{\alpha}},E]+[T_{H_{\alpha}},X]\in\mathfrak{h}.

(4)

Due to the ad-invariance of the inner product, we have
[T_{H_{\alpha}},E]\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
and
[T_{H_{\alpha}},X]\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\ominus X
. Since
\alpha(H_{\alpha})\neq 0
, we must have
X\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, we must have that
X\in\mathfrak{a}
.

It remains to show that
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
. Let
\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}
be any positive root and suppose that
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\subsetneq\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
. Take a nonzero vector
Y_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
orthogonal to
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
. Since
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\pi(\mathfrak{h})
, we can consider the vector
Y_{\lambda}+T_{Y_{\lambda}}\in\mathfrak{h}
. Moreover, there exists
H\in\mathfrak{a}\ominus X
such that
\lambda
takes nonzero value at
X+H
. We have:

[E+X+H+T_{E+X+H},Y_{\lambda}+T_{Y_{\lambda}}]=[E,T_{Y_{\lambda}}]+\lambda(X+H)Y_{\lambda}+[T_{E+X+H},Y_{\lambda}]\in\mathfrak{h}.

Note that
[E,T_{Y_{\lambda}}]\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
and
[T_{E+X+H},Y_{\lambda}]\in\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}\ominus Y_{\lambda}
. With respect to the decomposition
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{-}
, it is easy to see that
[E,T_{Y_{\lambda}}]\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
and
\lambda(X+H)Y_{\lambda}+[T_{E+X+H},Y_{\lambda}]\in\mathfrak{h}_{-}
. Since
\lambda(X+H)\neq 0
, the latter vector lies outside of
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
, which is a contradiction. We deduce that
\mathfrak{h}
contains
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
and, as
\lambda
was arbitrary,
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that, in light of Lemma
4.6
, the projection
\pi(\mathfrak{h})
of
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
is precisely the subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
described in Theorem
C
. The next theorem is the final step in our line of argument and it concludes the proof of Theorem
C
.

Theorem 4.7
.

The connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}=\pi(\mathfrak{h})
has the same orbits in
M_{0}\times M_{-}
as
\mathsf{H}
.

Proof.

Our goal is to construct a connected Lie subgroup
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
containing both
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
that acts with cohomogeneity one and thus has the same orbits as either of these two subgroups. We are going to define
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
as the connected Lie subgroup with Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\pi(\mathfrak{h})
, but first we need to prove that
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
is indeed a subalgebra, i.e., that both
\mathfrak{t}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
normalize
\pi(\mathfrak{h})
. Thanks to Lemma
4.6
, we know that

\pi(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)\oplus\mathbb{R}(E+X)\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\ominus X)\oplus\mathfrak{n}.

Since
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
is contained in
Z_{\mathfrak{k}_{-}}(\mathfrak{a})
, it commutes with
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
and normalizes
\mathfrak{n}
, hence it must normalize
\pi(\mathfrak{h})
.

In view of the
\ad
-invariance of the inner product, in order to show that
\mathfrak{t}_{0}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h})
normalizes
\pi(\mathfrak{h})
, it suffices to prove that it normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
. We will do this separately for
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}),\,\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}})
, and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{-})
. Consider the subalgebra
\widecheck{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.9599pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.9599pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[5.6321pt]{\raisebox{-0.40833pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[4.54321pt]{\raisebox{-0.29166pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})
and its corresponding connected Lie subgroup
\widecheck{\mathsf{H}}_{0}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Write
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{T}_{-}
for the connected Lie subgroups of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{-}
, respectively. Note that the latter subgroup is compact. Moreover, we have
\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\mathfrak{h}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}
and hence
\mathsf{H}_{0}
is the identity component of
\mathsf{H}\cap((\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{T}_{-})
. Since this intersection is a closed subgroup, so is
\mathsf{H}_{0}
. Observe that
\widecheck{\mathsf{H}}_{0}
is the image of
\mathsf{H}_{0}
under the projection
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{T}_{-}\to\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As
\mathsf{T}_{-}
is compact, this projection is a closed map. We deduce that
\widecheck{\mathsf{H}}_{0}
is a closed subgroup and thus its action on
M_{0}
is proper.

We would now like to show that this action has no singular orbits. Observe that we can think of it as the effectivization of the projection action
\mathsf{H}_{0}\curvearrowright M_{0}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that the latter action has discrete isotropy at every point. To this end, we first notice that the vector space sum
\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}
is easily seen to be a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}
. We write
\mathsf{H}_{0,\mathrm{mid}}\subseteq\mathsf{H}
for the corresponding connected Lie subgroup. Since
\mathsf{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathsf{H}_{0,\mathrm{mid}}
, it suffices to show that all isotropy subgroups of the projection action
\mathsf{H}_{0,\mathrm{mid}}\curvearrowright M_{0}
are discrete. Since the projection of
\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}
to
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
is surjective, the action
\mathsf{H}_{0,\mathrm{mid}}\curvearrowright M_{0}
has cohomogeneity 0 and is thus transitive. Consequently, we just need to show that its isotropy subgroup at
0
is discrete. To see this, just notice that
\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}}\subseteq(\mathfrak{t}_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus(\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{a})
has trivial intersection with
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
because its projection to
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
is a linear isomorphism. Altogether, we conclude that
\widecheck{\mathsf{H}}_{0}
is a closed connected subgroup of
\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
that acts on
M_{0}
without singular orbits. As
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{r}^{n}}(\widecheck{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})=\pi(\mathfrak{h}_{0})=\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
, this action has cohomogeneity one, so its orbits are a family of mutually parallel affine hyperplanes. This allows us to apply Remark
4.3
, which states that the projection
