\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\widecheck{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})
preserves
T_{0}(\widecheck{\mathsf{H}}_{0}\cdot 0)
, or in other words, normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
.

It remains to show that the same is true for
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}})
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{-})
. Given
V\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
, a straightforward computation yields

[E+X+T_{E+X},V+T_{V}]=[E,T_{V}]+[T_{E+X},V]\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\cap\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E.

Since
[E,T_{V}]
is orthogonal to
E
, so must be
[T_{E+X},V]
. Therefore,
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{mid}})=\mathbb{R}\hskip 1.0ptT_{E+X}
normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
. Similarly, if
H\in\mathfrak{a}\ominus X
, one has

[H+T_{H},V+T_{V}]=[T_{H},V]\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\cap\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E,

so
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h}_{-})
normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
. We conclude that
\mathfrak{t}_{0}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}(\mathfrak{h})
normalizes
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E
and thus the whole
\pi(\mathfrak{h})
, so
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathfrak{t}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{-}\oplus\pi(\mathfrak{h})
is a subalgebra of
(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
.

Consider the connected Lie subgroup
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
with Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
. It is clear from our construction that
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
, so the orbits of
\mathsf{H}
in
M_{0}\times M_{-}
are contained in those of
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
. Now,
T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\cong\pi(\mathfrak{h})=\pi(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}})\cong T_{o}(\widehat{\mathsf{H}}\cdot o)
, so
\mathsf{H}\cdot o=\widehat{\mathsf{H}}\cdot o
. This implies that
\mathsf{H}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
share the same orbits. Similarly, since
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}=\pi(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
are contained in the orbits of
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
. As
T_{o}(\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\cdot o)\cong\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}=\pi(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}})\cong T_{o}(\widehat{\mathsf{H}}\cdot o)
, we deduce that
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
has the same orbits as
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
and thus as
\mathsf{H}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.2.
The geometry of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}

Here we briefly outline the geometry of the orbits of the action of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. To begin with, we show that we can reduce the investigation to a single orbit. Indeed, note that the derived subalgebra of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
is precisely
\mathfrak{n}
. Since
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
. Therefore, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
are mutually congruent (see
[
KT13
, Lem. 2.1]
), so we can fully understand the geometry of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
by only studying the geometry of the orbit through
o
. Note that, under the identification
M_{0}\times M_{-}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
, the orbit
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\cdot o
simply becomes
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
. The vector
\xi=\frac{||E||^{2}X-||X||^{2}E}{||E||||X||\sqrt{||E||^{2}+||X||^{2}}}
, thought of as a left-invariant vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
, is then a unit normal field to
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\xi}
the shape operator of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
with respect to
\xi
, and let
\nabla
denote the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
. Note that
\ad_{\xi}
is a self-adjoint endomorphism of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n})
. The Koszul formula, combined with the left-invariance of the metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
, yields

2\langle\mathcal{A}_{\xi}V,W\rangle=2\langle-\nabla_{V}\xi,W\rangle=-\langle[\xi,V],W\rangle+\langle[V,W],\xi\rangle+\langle[\xi,W],V\rangle=2\langle\ad_{\xi}V,W\rangle

for every
V,W\in\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}
. Consequently, the shape operator
\mathcal{A}_{\xi}
is simply given by
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\ad_{\xi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}}}
. It is straightforward to check that
\xi
commutes with
V_{0}=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus E)\oplus\mathbb{R}(E+X)\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\ominus X)
, which implies that
V_{0}
is contained in the principal curvature space of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
with principal curvature
0
. Similarly,
\ad_{\xi}Y_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha(X)||E||}{||X||\sqrt{||E||^{2}+||X||^{2}}}Y_{\alpha}
for any positive root
\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}
and
Y_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. Thus, for each such
\alpha
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
is contained in the principal curvature space with principal curvature
\frac{\alpha(X)||E||}{||X||\sqrt{||E||^{2}+||X||^{2}}}
. We round up the above discussion with the following

Proposition 4.8
.

The orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
in
M_{0}\times M_{-}
are mutually congruent.
The shape operator of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\cdot o
with respect to
\xi
coincides with
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\ad_{\xi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{h}_{E,X}}}
. The mean curvature of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}\cdot o
is given by

\frac{||E||}{||X||\sqrt{||E||^{2}+||X||^{2}}}\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\alpha(X).

In particular, the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
are minimal if and only if
\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\alpha(X)=0
.

Remark 4.9
.

It should be noted that the geometry of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
is analogous to that of the orbits of the action
\mathsf{H}_{X}\curvearrowright M_{-}
described in item (b) of Theorem
C
. Namely,
[
BT03
, Prop. 3.1]
shows that the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{X}
are mutually congruent and have mean curvature
\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\alpha(X)
. Note that if the rank of
M_{-}
is
\geq 2
, we can always find a nonzero vector
X_{0}\in\mathfrak{a}
with
\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\alpha(X_{0})=0
. Recall that a foliation on a Riemannian manifold is said to be harmonic if all of its orbits are minimal. In the context of Theorem
C
, the actions given by
I(M_{+})\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathsf{G}_{-},\,\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{X_{0}}
, and
\mathsf{G}_{+}\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X_{0}}
(where
X_{0}
is as above) give rise to non-congruent harmonic foliations on
M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
.

5.
Actions with singular orbits on symmetric spaces of nonpositive curvature

In this section, we prove Theorem
D
, which states that a cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
with a singular orbit must necessarily decompose. We are going to argue by contradiction. Recall that the assumption that the action of
\mathsf{H}
does not decompose implies that the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are transitive. This naturally leads us to transitive isometric actions on Euclidean spaces, which we discuss in Subsection
5.1
. For a symmetric space of noncompact type
M_{-}=\mathsf{G}_{-}/\mathsf{K}_{-}
, the maximal proper connected Lie subgroups of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
acting on
M_{-}
transitively are precisely the (identity components of the) maximal proper parabolic subgroups. In Subsection
5.2
, we extend some results of
[
BT13
]
to spaces of the form
M_{0}\times M_{-}
, showing that if
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(\mathfrak{h})
is contained in some maximal parabolic subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
, then the action of
\mathsf{H}
can be obtained by extending some cohomogeneity-one action on a lower rank symmetric space. Finally, in Subsection
5.3
, we conclude with the proofs of Theorems
D
and
A
.

5.1.
Transitive actions on Euclidean spaces.

We now take a look at transitive groups of isometries of Euclidean spaces: we recall some structural results and turn them into a complete and explicit description of such groups, which is going to be crucial for the proof of Theorem
D
. Such actions have been extensively studied in the literature in the much more general context of homogeneous Hadamard manifolds and Riemannian solvmanifolds (see
[
Ale75
,
AW76
,
GW88
]
). For instance, Alekseevskii gave an explicit description of simply transitive groups of isometries of Euclidean spaces in
[
Ale75
, Prop. 3.1]
. For any Hadamard manifold
N
and a Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I(N)
acting transitively on
N
, there exists a solvable Lie subgroup
\mathsf{F}\subseteq\mathsf{H}
whose action on
N
is simply transitive. In the special case of
N\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one can actually take
\mathsf{F}
to be a normal subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
such that
\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{H}_{p}\cdot\mathsf{F}
for some point
p\in N
. Combining these results together, one can arrive at a description of all transitive groups of isometries of the Euclidean space. However, below we include a proof of the latter result concerning the existence of a simply transitive subgroup in the special case of Euclidean spaces in order to clarify some technical details: for instance, it is shown in
[
Ale75
, Th. 1.1]
that, for any homogeneous Riemannian manifold
N
of nonpositive curvature, one can always find a ‘‘normal transitive”
3
3

3

Note that the notion of “normal transitive” subgroup in
[
Ale75
]
is nonstandard: a “normal transitive” subgroup of
I^{0}(N)
is not actually a normal subgroup unless the Levi factor of
I^{0}(N)
is compact.
subgroup
\mathsf{F}
of
I^{0}(N)
. One could try to use the same approach to find a normal transitive subgroup of any transitive group of isometries
\mathsf{H}
of
N
, however, there is a caveat: the proof of
[
Ale75
, Th. 1.1]
uses the fact that the isotropy groups of points
p\in N
are maximal compact subgroups, which may not be true for
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright N
if the action of
\mathsf{H}
is non-proper (that is, if
\mathsf{H}
is not closed in
I(N)
). This same closedness assumption seems to be implicitly made in the proof of
[
AW76
, Prop. 2.5]
. In the special case when
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I^{0}(N)
is solvable, one can apply
[
GW88
, Lem. 1.2]
, whose proof avoids this technical problem. By combining these approaches for the Euclidean space, we arrive at the following

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}=I^{0}(M_{0})
be a (not necessarily closed) connected Lie subgroup acting transitively on the Euclidean space
M_{0}
.
Then, there exists a connected normal solvable Lie subgroup
\mathsf{F}
of
\mathsf{H}
that acts on
M_{0}
simply transitively.

Proof.

Fix a Levi-Malcev decomposition
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{l}\ltimes\mathfrak{rad(h)}
and let
\mathsf{L}
and
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
be the corresponding connected Lie subgroups of
\mathsf{H}
. Here
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
is a closed normal solvable Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
,
\mathsf{L}
is semisimple, and the intersection
\mathsf{L}\cap\mathsf{Rad(H)}
is discrete. We claim that the Levi factor
\mathfrak{l}
is compact. Indeed, assume that
\mathfrak{l}
has a noncompact ideal
\mathfrak{l}_{i}
. The image of
\mathfrak{l}_{i}
under the projection map
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}\colon\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n}\to\mathfrak{so}_{n}
(which is a homomorphism since
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
is an ideal) is a noncompact semisimple subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
, so it must be trivial. In other words,
\mathfrak{l}_{i}
must be contained in
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and is thus abelian, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\mathfrak{l}
is a compact semisimple Lie algebra and thus
\mathsf{L}
is a compact subgroup. By Cartan’s fixed point theorem,
\mathsf{L}
must have a fixed point
p\in M_{0}
. We may assume, possibly after conjugating the Levi factor by an element of
\mathsf{H}
, that
p=0
and thus
\mathsf{L}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}
. Moreover,
M_{0}=\mathsf{H}\cdot 0=\mathsf{Rad(H)}\cdot(\mathsf{L}\cdot 0)=\mathsf{Rad(H)}\cdot 0
, so
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
acts on
M_{0}
transitively.

Consider now the isotropy subgroup
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{Rad(H)}\cap\mathsf{SO}_{n}
of
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
at
0
and let
\mathsf{Nil(H)}
be the nilradical of
\mathsf{H}
(i.e., the maximal connected normal nilpotent Lie subgroup). Note that
\mathsf{C}
may fail to be a closed subgroup of
\mathsf{SO}_{n}
because we did not require
\mathsf{H}
to be closed either. We claim that the intersection
\mathsf{C}\cap\mathsf{Nil(H)}
is trivial. Indeed, note that the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{Nil(H)}
is the nilradical
\mathfrak{nil(h)}
of
\mathfrak{h}
. For every element
X\in\mathfrak{nil(h)}
, the operator
\ad_{\mathfrak{rad(h)}}(X)
is nilpotent. By Engel’s theorem, there exists a basis for
\mathfrak{rad(h)}
such that
\ad_{\mathfrak{rad(h)}}(X)
is strictly upper-triangular for every
X\in\mathfrak{nil(h)}
. In that same basis,
\Ad_{\mathsf{Rad(H)}}(g)
is upper-triangular with ones on the diagonal for every
g\in\mathsf{Nil(H)}
. In particular, the only possible complex eigenvalue of
\Ad_{\mathsf{Rad(H)}}(g)
is 1. On the other hand, note that the
\Ad_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\ltimes\mathfrak{r}^{n}
restricts to an
\Ad_{\mathsf{C}}
-invariant inner product on
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
, and thus
\Ad_{\mathsf{Rad(H)}}(g)
is complex diagonalizable for every
g\in\mathsf{C}
. We conclude that
\Ad_{\mathsf{Rad(H)}}(g)
is the identity for every
\mathsf{C}\cap\mathsf{Nil(H)}
, hence
\mathsf{C}\cap\mathsf{Nil(H)}\subseteq\mathsf{Z(Rad(H))}
. Since
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
acts on
M_{0}
transitively, any element of
\mathsf{Nil(H)}\cap\mathsf{SO}_{n}
has to fix every point of
M_{0}
and thus be trivial. We conclude that
\mathsf{Nil(H)}\cap\mathsf{SO}_{n}=\{e\}
.

Now, choose a subspace
\mathfrak{f}
complementary to
\mathfrak{c}
in
\mathfrak{rad(h)}
such that
\mathfrak{nil(h)}\subseteq\mathfrak{f}
(this is possible since
\mathfrak{c}\cap\mathfrak{nil(h)}=\{0\}
), and let
\mathsf{F}
the corresponding connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{Rad(H)}
. Thanks to
[
Kna02
, Prop. 1.41]
, we have
[\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{h}]\subseteq[\mathfrak{rad(h)},\mathfrak{h}]\subseteq\mathfrak{nil(h)}\subseteq\mathfrak{f}
, so
\mathfrak{f}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{h}
and thus
\mathsf{F}
is a normal subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
. In particular, we have
\mathfrak{rad(h)}=\mathfrak{c}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{f}
and thus
\mathsf{Rad(H)}=\mathsf{C}\cdot\mathsf{F}
. By construction,
\mathsf{C}
fixes the origin, and so
M_{0}=\mathsf{Rad(H)}\cdot 0=\mathsf{F}\cdot(\mathsf{C}\cdot 0)=\mathsf{F}\cdot 0
. We deduce that
\mathsf{F}
is a normal solvable connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
that acts on
M_{0}
transitively. Since
\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}=\{0\}
, we actually have that
\mathsf{F}
is simply connected and closed in
I(M_{0})
and its action on
M_{0}
is simply transitive (see
[
AW76
, Lem. 2.4.]
).
∎

Remark 5.2
.

It follows from the proof that
\mathsf{H}
splits as a semidirect product
\mathsf{H}=(\mathsf{H}\cap\mathsf{SO}_{n})\ltimes\mathsf{F}
.
Thus, we can recover any connected Lie subgroup of
I(M_{0})
acting on
M_{0}
transitively from its simply transitive part
\mathsf{F}
by taking the semidirect product of
\mathsf{F}
with some Lie subgroup of the normalizer
N_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}(\mathsf{F})
.

In
[
Ale75
]
, Alekseevskii gives the following explicit description of simply transitive actions on Euclidean spaces:

Lemma 5.3

(
[
Ale75
, Prop. 3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}^{n}=\mathfrak{v}\oplus\mathfrak{w}
be an orthogonal decomposition of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
, and let
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{v}\to\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})\subseteq\mathfrak{so}_{n}
be an injective Lie algebra homomorphism. Consider the subalgebra
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
of
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\ltimes\mathfrak{r^{n}}
, where
\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}=\{v+\varphi(v)\mid v\in\mathfrak{v}\}
. Then the connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{F}
of
\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}
acts on
M_{0}
simply transitively. Conversely, a connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{F}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
acting on
M_{0}
simply transitively has Lie algebra of the form
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
for some injective homomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{v}\to\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})
, where
\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus\mathfrak{w}
.

Remark 5.4
.

Notice that the image of
\varphi
is an abelian subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so(w)}
, hence we have
\dim(\mathfrak{v})\leq\operatorname{rank}(\mathfrak{so(w)})=\lfloor\frac{n-\dim(\mathfrak{v})}{2}\rfloor
, or equivalently,
\dim(\mathfrak{v})\leq\frac{n}{3}
.

We are now ready to give a complete description of all transitive actions on
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie subgroup of
I(M_{0})
whose action on
M_{0}
is transitive. Thanks to Lemma
5.1
and Remark
5.2
, we can write
\mathsf{H}=(\mathsf{H}\cap\mathsf{SO}_{n})\ltimes\mathsf{F}
, where
\mathsf{F}
acts on
M_{0}
simply transitively. By virtue of Lemma
5.3
,
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
, where
\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{f}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{n}
,
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus\mathfrak{w}
, and
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{v}\to\mathfrak{so(w)}
is an injective Lie algebra homomorphism. In the proof of Lemma
5.1
, we saw that
[\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{f}]\subseteq\mathfrak{nil}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq\mathfrak{f}
. We must then have
\mathfrak{nil}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq\mathfrak{nil}(\mathfrak{f})
, and the latter nilradical is easily seen to coincide with
\mathfrak{w}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
preserves
\mathfrak{w}
. Since the inner product on
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n}
is
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
-invariant, we see that
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
must then preserve
\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}=\mathfrak{f}\ominus\mathfrak{w}
as well. We deduce that
[\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n},\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}]=\{0\}
. This is only possible when
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
commutes with the projections of
\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}
in both
\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})
and
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
, which are equal to
\varphi(\mathfrak{v})
and
\mathfrak{v}
, respectively. But
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
can only commute with
\mathfrak{v}
when it is contained in
\mathfrak{so(w)}
. We conclude that
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
is contained in
Z_{\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})}(\varphi(\mathfrak{v}))
. Finally, note that
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{n}=\mathfrak{w}
implies that
\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}
intersects
\varphi(\mathfrak{v})
trivially. Rewriting
\mathfrak{h}=((\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n})\oplus\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
, one can think of these three summands as the rotational, screw-motion, and translational parts of
\mathfrak{h}
, respectively.

Conversely, in order to construct a transitive group of isometries of
M_{0}
, start with an orthogonal splitting
\mathfrak{r}^{n}=\mathfrak{v}\oplus\mathfrak{w}
and a subalgebra
\mathfrak{c}\subseteq\mathfrak{so(w)}
. Split
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{z(c)}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{ss}}
into its center and semisimple part and split
\mathfrak{z(c)}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{b}
so that
\dim(\mathfrak{a})=\dim(\mathfrak{v})
. Finally, pick any isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{v}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}\mathfrak{a}
. Then the connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
with Lie algebra
((\mathfrak{b}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{ss}})\oplus\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
acts on
M_{0}
transitively.

5.2.
The canonical extension procedure on
M_{0}\times M_{-}

In
[
BT13
]
Berndt, together with the third author, introduced two methods for constructing cohomogeneity-one actions on a given symmetric space
M_{-}=\mathsf{G}_{-}/\mathsf{K}_{-}
of noncompact type: the
canonical extension
and the
nilpotent construction
. Moreover, they showed that if
\mathsf{Q}
is a proper parabolic subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
and
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{Q}
is a closed connected subgroup acting on
M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and a singular orbit, then the action of
\mathsf{H}
is always orbit-equivalent to an action constructed by one of these procedures.

For reasons that will become evident below (see Theorem
5.7
), in this subsection we will focus on the canonical extension method. In its original formulation, this method allows one to extend any isometric action
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\curvearrowright B_{\Phi}
on a boundary component
B_{\Phi}
of
M_{-}
to an isometric action on
M_{-}
of the same cohomogeneity. This procedure was later generalized in
[
Dom15
]
to a larger class of Riemannian manifolds endowed with a
polar action of a specific type. We apply that generalized method in the special case when the ambient space is
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. By adapting the arguments of
[
BT13
]
, we show that if
\mathsf{Q}_{j}
is a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
and
\mathsf{H}\subseteq(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{Q}_{j}
acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and a singular orbit and its action does not decompose, then it is orbit-equivalent to some action obtained by canonical extension from
M_{0}\times B_{j}
. This result will prove instrumental in the proof of Theorem
D
.

We begin by introducing the canonical extension method on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. Let
\mathfrak{q}_{\Phi}
be the parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
associated with a subset of simple roots
\Phi\subseteq\Lambda
, and consider the corresponding horospherical decomposition

M_{-}\cong B_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
properly and isometrically. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathsf{H}
is a closed subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{0}\times B_{\Phi})=I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
and then lift it to
I^{0}(M_{0})\times\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
(recall from Subsection
2.4
that the subgroup
\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}\subseteq\mathsf{G}_{-}
is a finite covering of
I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
). Altogether, we assume that
\mathsf{H}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{0})\times\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}
.
Since
\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}
normalizes
\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
, we have that

\mathfrak{h}^{\Lambda}=(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}

is a subalgebra of
(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
. We write
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
for the corresponding connected Lie subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
. With respect to the Langlands decomposition, one has

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}=(\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi})\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle\subseteq(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times((\mathsf{G}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi})\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi})

\displaystyle\subseteq(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times((\mathsf{M}_{\Phi}\times\mathsf{A}_{\Phi})\ltimes\mathsf{N}_{\Phi})=I^{0}(M_{0})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}.

In particular, this shows that
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
is a closed subgroup and thus acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
properly. By construction,
\mathfrak{h}^{\Lambda}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{-})=\mathfrak{h}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi}\cap\mathfrak{k}_{-}))
. Moreover, if we identify
T_{o}(M_{0}\times M_{-})\cong\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
and
T_{o}(M_{0}\times B_{\Phi})\cong\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
, then it is easy to see that the normal space to
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}\cdot o
at
o
in
M_{0}\times M_{-}
coincides with that to
\mathsf{H}\cdot o
at
o
in
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
. Therefore, both actions have the same slice representation at
o
(on the level of Lie algebras), and consequently, the same cohomogeneity. We refer to the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
as the
canonical extension
of
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
.

Remark 5.5
.

If
\mathsf{H}
is a connected Lie group acting on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
isometrically but not properly, we can still define its canonical extension in the exact same way, the only difference being that the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
may fail to be proper. Using the horospherical decomposition, one can readily see that the orbits of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
are properly embedded if and only if such are the orbits of
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
. Consequently, if we know that a proper isometric action on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
is orbit-equivalent to the canonical extension of an isometric action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
, we may assume without loss of generality that the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
is also proper.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
be as above, and let
B_{\Phi}
be a boundary component of
M_{-}
. Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. Then, the canonical extension
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
decomposes if and only if
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
does.

Proof.

Suppose that the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
decomposes. Then, it has the same orbits as some product action
\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
, where one of the actions
\mathsf{H}_{0}\curvearrowright M_{0},\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\curvearrowright B_{\Phi}
is of cohomogeneity one and the other one is transitive. Therefore,
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
and
(\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{\Phi})^{\Lambda}
also have the same orbits (see
[
BT13
, Prop. 4.2]
, or
[
Dom15
, Th. 2.1(vii)]
). Note that the Lie algebra of
(\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{\Phi})^{\Lambda}
can be written as

\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\left((\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}\right)=\mathfrak{h}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi}^{\Lambda},

(5)

where
\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi}^{\Lambda}=(\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
is precisely the Lie algebra of the Lie subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}^{\Lambda}
of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
obtained by canonical extension from
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
(in the original sense of
[
BT13
]
). Observe also that the right-hand side of (
5
) is a direct sum of Lie algebras. It follows that the action of
(\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{\Phi})^{\Lambda}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
has the same orbits as the product action
\mathsf{H}_{0}\times\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}^{\Lambda}
, so it decomposes.

In order to prove the converse, suppose that the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
does not decompose. By Lemma
3.3
,
\mathsf{H}
must act transitively on both
M_{0}
and
B_{\Phi}
(by means of the projection actions). Under the identification
T_{o_{M_{-}}}B_{\Phi}\cong\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
, this means that
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{r}^{n}}(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}}(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\Lambda}
, we know that the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
on
M_{0}
must be transitive as well. Since
\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\Lambda}
, we also have

\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}}(\mathfrak{h}^{\Lambda})=\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}.

Since
T_{o_{M_{-}}}M_{-}\cong\mathfrak{b}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\Phi}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\Phi}
, the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
on
M_{-}
is also transitive. By invoking Lemma
3.3
once again, we see that the action of
\mathsf{H}^{\Lambda}
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
does not decompose.
∎

The following is the main result of this subsection:

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\mathsf{H}\subset(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
be a closed connected subgroup acting on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one and a singular orbit, and suppose that its action does not decompose. Let
\alpha_{j}\in\Lambda
be a simple root of
M_{-}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{h}
is contained in
(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{j}
, where
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
is the maximal proper parabolic subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
associated to
\Lambda\setminus\{\alpha_{j}\}
. Then the action of
\mathsf{H}
has the same orbits as the canonical extension of some proper cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}_{j}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{j}
.

This theorem is largely an adaptation of
[
BT13
, Sect. 5]
, so much so that the original proof mostly applies verbatim with only minor and obvious modifications. For this reason, we are not going to give a full proof: rather, we will formulate the key lemmas and intermediate results and comment on how and where the proof from
[
BT13
]
needs to be modified.

To begin with, note that we could have asked
\mathfrak{h}
to be contained in
(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{q}
for any maximal proper parabolic subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
because any such
\mathfrak{q}
is inner-conjugate to
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
for some
\alpha_{j}\in\Lambda
. We may also assume that the singular orbit of
\mathsf{H}
passes through the base point
o=(0,o_{M_{-}})
because
\mathsf{Q}_{j}
acts on
M_{-}
transitively.

As already mentioned, the horospherical decomposition allows us to identify
M_{0}\times M_{-}\cong M_{0}\times B_{j}\times\mathsf{A}_{j}\times\mathsf{N}_{j}
and thus
T_{o}(M_{0}\times M_{-})\cong\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. If we decompose
\mathfrak{q}_{j}=\mathfrak{k}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and write
\tau\colon(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{j}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}
for the natural projection, we have
T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\cong\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. The slice representation of
\mathsf{H}
at
o
takes the form

\mathsf{H}\cap(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\times\mathsf{K}_{-})\to\mathsf{O}(\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)),\quad k\mapsto{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Ad(k)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)}},

where we think of the normal space as a subspace of
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. We know that
\mathsf{H}\cap(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\times\mathsf{K}_{-})\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\times\mathsf{K}_{j}
and the latter subgroup centralizes
\mathfrak{a}_{j}
. Since
\dim\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\geq 2
and the slice representation acts transitively on the unit sphere in
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
, we must have
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and thus
\mathfrak{a}_{j}\subseteq\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. Recall that we have a grading
\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{k}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{0}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j})\ominus(\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j})),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{k}\ominus(\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{k}),\;\text{for }k=1,\ldots,m.

By design, we have

\nu_{o}(H\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{w}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{w}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{w}_{m}.

(6)

The main distinction from
[
BT13
]
here is that the authors of that paper had no
M_{0}
factor, so their definition of
\mathfrak{w}_{0}
did not involve
\mathfrak{r}^{n}
. The following lemma can be proven in exactly the same way as in
[
BT13
]
by throwing in
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
and
\mathsf{SO}_{n}
wherever necessary.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
X\in\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
be nonzero and write
\pi_{l}\colon\bigoplus_{k=0}^{m}\mathfrak{w}_{k}\rightarrow\mathfrak{w}_{l}
for the natural projection. Then,

(a)

\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)=\mathbb{R}X\oplus[\mathfrak{h}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{j}),\hskip 1.0ptX]
.

(b)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\pi_{k}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)}}\colon\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\to\mathfrak{w}_{k}
is surjective and
\mathsf{H}\cap(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\times\mathsf{K}_{j})
-equivariant for each
k=0,1,\ldots,m
.

(c)

If
\mathfrak{w}_{k}\neq 0
, then
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\pi_{k}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)}}\colon\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\to\mathfrak{w}_{k}
is an isomorphism. In particular,
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
is an irreducible
\mathsf{H}\cap(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\times\mathsf{K}_{j})
-module.

Recall that
H^{j}\in\mathfrak{a}_{j}\subseteq\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. This means that there exists
H\in\mathfrak{h}
such that
\tau(H)=H^{j}
. We call such
H
a
lift
of
H^{j}
. We can always write
H=H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
, where the first two summands are in
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{k}_{j}
, respectively. Notice that
\tau
restricts to a linear isomorphism between
\mathfrak{h}\ominus[\mathfrak{h}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{j})]
and
\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. If a lift
H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
is contained in
\mathfrak{h}\ominus[\mathfrak{h}\cap(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{j})]
, we call it a
perfect lift
. Plainly, there exists a unique perfect lift of
H^{j}
. Again, due to the absence of the
M_{0}
factor, lifts of
H^{j}
looked simply like
H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
in
[
BT13
]
. It should be noted that only perfect lifts were considered in that paper. We will come back to this later (see the discussion of the proof of Lemma
5.11
).

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
be the perfect lift of
H^{j}
, and denote
f=\ad(H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j})
. Then, for
k=0,1,\ldots,m
, we have:

(a)

f
normalizes
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
,

(b)

f^{2}=-c_{k}^{2}\Id
on
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
for some
c_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The proof of this lemma also goes essentially identically to the one in
[
BT13
]
. For example, in (a), one reduces the problem to showing that
f
preserves
\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j})
and
\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{k}
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
. One takes a vector
X
in either of these subspaces and picks
X_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}\in\mathfrak{so}_{n}
and
X_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}\in\mathfrak{k}_{j}
such that
X_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}+X_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}+X\in\mathfrak{h}
. One then brackets
X_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}+X_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}+X
with
H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
and deduces that
f(X)
must lie in
\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j})
or
\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{k}
, respectively. The proof of (b) carries over with similar adjustments. By tracing the proof, one sees that the condition on the lift
H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}+H^{j}
to be perfect is necessary in (b) but is not actually required in (a).

The following lemma sets strong restrictions on the normal space to the singular orbit
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
and is the chief place where our line of argument differs substantially from
[
BT13
]
:

Lemma 5.10
.

We have
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}
.

In other words, this lemma asserts that
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\subseteq\tau(\mathfrak{h})
, or equivalently,
\mathfrak{w}_{k}=\{0\}
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
. The main difference with
[
BT13
]
is that there is a second possibility there:
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{w}_{1}
, or equivalently,
\mathfrak{w}_{k}=\{0\}
for
k=0
and
k>1
. To see why this scenario does not arise in our case, let us first go through the proof. Surprisingly, most of the proof goes identically to the one in
[
BT13
]
with only minor modifications similar to those we did in the proof of Lemma
5.9
. One shows that either
\mathfrak{w}_{1}=\{0\}
, in which case
\mathfrak{w}_{k}=\{0\}
for each
k\geq 1
, or else
\mathfrak{w}_{1}\neq\{0\}
, in which case
\mathfrak{w}_{k}=\{0\}
for
k\neq 1
. To rule out the second option in our case, note that it is equivalent to
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{w}_{1}
. If this was the case, we would have
T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\cong\tau(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j}\oplus(\mathfrak{n}_{j}\ominus\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o))
. That would mean that the projection of
\tau(\mathfrak{h})
to
T_{o_{M_{-}}}(M_{-})
is not surjective and thus the projection action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M_{-}
is not transitive. By Lemma
3.3
, this would imply that the action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
decomposes, leading to a contradiction. This second possibility for
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
can only be ruled out here because we assume that the action does not decompose. In the original proof in
[
BT13
]
, this assumption did not make sense (due to the lack of the
M_{0}
factor). By tracing the proof in
[
BT13
]
, we see that the case
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{b}_{j}
led to the canonical extension, while
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{w}_{1}
led to the nilpotent construction. In our case, the second scenario does not arise. Instead, we only have
\nu_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)\subseteq\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{j}
, which is going to lead to (our version of) the canonical extension.

The next lemma sheds light on how the subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
interacts with the decomposition
{(\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})}\oplus((\mathfrak{m}_{j}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{j})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}_{j})
:

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
H^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}
be any lift of
H^{j}
. We have:

(a)

\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\tau(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}
.

(b)

\mathfrak{h}=[\mathfrak{h}\cap((\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{m}_{j})]\oplus\mathbb{R}(H^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j})\oplus[\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}]
.

In
[
BT13
]
, part (b) of this lemma reads as
\mathfrak{h}=[\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{j}]\oplus\mathbb{R}(H^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j})\oplus[\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{j}]
. The proof here is essentially the same as in
[
BT13
]
—with the same minor modifications as before. There is a small issue in the proof given in
[
BT13
]
that we would like to address and correct here. The proof starts by finding a connected solvable Lie subgroup
\mathsf{S}
of
\mathsf{H}
such that
\mathsf{S}\cdot o=\mathsf{H}\cdot o
. Then, the authors claim that the perfect lift of
H^{j}
lies in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}
of
\mathsf{S}
, which may not actually be the case (especially due to the freedom in the choice of
\mathsf{S}
). Fortunately, the property of the lift being perfect is not actually needed in the proof, and one can always find at least some lift of
H^{j}
in
\mathfrak{s}
: indeed, the condition
\mathsf{S}\cdot o=\mathsf{H}\cdot o
implies
T_{o}(\mathsf{S}\cdot o)=T_{o}(\mathsf{H}\cdot o)
, which translates to
\tau(\mathfrak{s})=\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. Since the latter contains
H^{j}
, we can find a lift of
H^{j}
that lies not only in
\mathfrak{h}
but in
\mathfrak{s}
. The rest of the proof goes unchanged. Finally, note that if part (b) of the lemma is true for some lift of
H^{j}
, then it is true for any lift.

We can immediately strengthen Lemma
5.11
by combining it with Lemma
5.10
:

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
H^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j}
be any lift of
H^{j}
. We have:

\mathfrak{h}=[\mathfrak{h}\cap((\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{m}_{j})]\oplus\mathbb{R}(H^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{so}_{n}}^{j}+H_{\mathfrak{k}_{j}}^{j})\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{j}.

Note that this corollary is specific to our situation and its analog does note have to hold in general in
[
BT13
]
because they may not have
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\subseteq\tau(\mathfrak{h})
. The last lemma rounds up the argument and completes the proof of Theorem
5.7
:

Lemma 5.13
.

The action of
\mathsf{H}
has the same orbits as the canonical extension of the cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}_{j}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{j}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{j}
is the connected Lie subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{M}_{j}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{j}=\mathfrak{h}\cap((\mathfrak{so}_{n}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{r}^{n})\oplus\mathfrak{m}_{j})
.

The proof of this lemma carries over almost verbatim from the proofs of Lemma 5.5 and Proposition 5.6 in
[
BT13
]
—again, with the same standard modification as before.

5.3.
The proof of Theorem
D

We now prove that a cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
that has a singular orbit necessarily decomposes. Our approach is going to be as follows. First, by employing Theorem
5.7
and replacing
M_{-}
with a suitable boundary component of
M_{-}
if necessary, we argue that we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{H}
projects surjectively onto
\mathsf{G}_{-}
. After that, we construct a chain of connected Lie subgroups

\cdots\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i+1}\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{1}\subsetneq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that
\mathsf{E}_{i}\curvearrowright M_{0}
is transitive and
\mathsf{H}\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
for every
i
. We then argue that this chain can be extended indefinitely, which leads to a contradiction for dimension reasons. We begin with the following technical lemma:

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\mathsf{E}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
be any connected Lie subgroup acting on
M_{0}
transitively, and let
\mathsf{E}
be a maximal proper connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
. Suppose that the projection actions of
\mathsf{E}
on
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are both transitive.
Then, either
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\mathsf{Q}^{0}
for some maximal proper parabolic subgroup
\mathsf{Q}\subsetneq\mathsf{G}_{-}
, or else
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{i+1}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
for some maximal proper connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{E}_{i+1}
of
\mathsf{E}_{i}
whose action on
M_{0}
is transitive. In either case,
\mathsf{E}
acts on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
transitively.

Proof.

Consider the natural projections
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{E}_{i}}\colon\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}\to\mathsf{E}_{i}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}\colon\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}\to\mathsf{G}_{-}
. If
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{E}_{i}}(\mathsf{E})\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i}
, by the maximality of
\mathsf{E}
, we must have that
\mathsf{E}_{i+1}:=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{E}_{i}}(\mathsf{E})
is a maximal proper connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{E}_{i}
and
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{i+1}\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
. Since by hypothesis the projection action of
\mathsf{E}
on
M_{0}
is transitive, so must be the action of
\mathsf{E}_{i+1}
.

Similarly, if
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{E})\subsetneq\mathsf{G}_{-}
, then
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{E})
is a maximal proper connected Lie subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
and
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{i}\times\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{E})
. Such a subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
must be either reductive
4
4

4

This notion of reductive subgroup is stronger than just being reductive as a Lie group (see the discussion on page 6 in
[
Kol11
]
).
or the identity component of a parabolic subgroup (see
[
Mos61
]
). If
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{E})
is reductive, it cannot act on
M_{-}
transitively (see
[
BT13
, Prop. 3.1]
), implying that the projection action of
\mathsf{E}
on
M_{-}
is not transitive. Thus,
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{E})
must be the identity component of some maximal proper parabolic subgroup
\mathsf{Q}
of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
.

From now on, we assume that
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{E}_{i}}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}
restrict to surjective homomorphisms from
\mathsf{E}
onto
\mathsf{E}_{i}
and
\mathsf{G}_{-}
, respectively. Let
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{e}_{i}}\colon\mathfrak{e}\to\mathfrak{e}_{i}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}\colon\mathfrak{e}\to\mathfrak{g}_{-}
be the corresponding surjective homomorphisms at the level of Lie algebras. Observe that
\mathfrak{e}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
: for any
X_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{e}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{-}
, we can find some
X\in\mathfrak{e}
such that
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}(X)=X_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}}
, and so

[X_{\mathfrak{g}_{-}},Y]=[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{e}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{-}.

Since
\mathfrak{g}_{-}
is semisimple, we can split it into a direct sum
\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k}
of simple noncompact ideals. Without loss of generality, we may assume that

\mathfrak{e}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{s}

for some
s\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
(where by
s=0
we mean that
\mathfrak{e}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{-}=\{0\}
). Note that it is not possible to have
s=k
because that would imply
\mathfrak{g}_{-}\subseteq\mathfrak{e}
and, since
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{e}_{i}}(\mathfrak{e})=\mathfrak{e}_{i}
, also
\mathfrak{e_{i}}\subseteq\mathfrak{e}
. This would contradict the fact that
\mathfrak{s}
is a proper subalgebra of
\mathfrak{e}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}
.

The projection
\mathfrak{e}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}\to\mathfrak{e}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{s}
along
\mathfrak{g}_{s+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k}
(which is a Lie algebra homomorphism) restricts to an isomorphism

\mathfrak{e}\xrightarrow{\Psi}\mathfrak{e}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{s},

since
\mathfrak{e}
projects surjectively onto
\mathfrak{e}_{i}
and the kernel of
\Psi
is
\mathfrak{e}\cap(\mathfrak{g}_{s+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k})=\{0\}
.

It follows from our discussion in Subsection
5.1
that we can write
\mathfrak{e}_{i}=((\mathfrak{e}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n})\oplus\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{w}
, where
\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{e}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{r}^{n}
,
\mathfrak{e}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})
,
\mathfrak{v}=\mathfrak{r}^{n}\ominus\mathfrak{w}
, and
\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi}\subseteq\mathfrak{so}(\mathfrak{w})\oplus\mathfrak{v}
. Note that
\Psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{w})
is an abelian ideal of
\mathfrak{e}
. Since the restriction of the projection
\Phi\colon\mathfrak{e}_{i}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}\to\mathfrak{g}_{s+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k}
to
\mathfrak{e}
is a surjective homomorphism,
\Phi(\Psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{w}))
is an abelian ideal of
\mathfrak{g}_{s+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k}
, hence it must be trivial. It follows that
\Psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{w})=\mathfrak{w}
, or in other words,
\mathfrak{w}\subseteq\mathfrak{e}
. Consequently, the restriction of
\Phi
to
\Psi^{-1}((\mathfrak{e}_{i}\cap\mathfrak{so}_{n})\oplus\mathfrak{v}^{\varphi})
must be surjective. But the latter is a compact Lie algebra, whereas
\mathfrak{g}_{s+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k}
is noncompact semisimple, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

We can now proceed to prove Theorem
D
:

Proof of Theorem
D
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a closed connected subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{G}_{-}
whose action on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
is of cohomogeneity one and has a singular orbit. We assume that the action does not decompose and are seeking a contradiction. By Lemma
3.3
, this is equivalent to saying that the projection actions of
\mathsf{H}
on both
M_{0}
and
M_{-}
are transitive.

As mentioned in the proof of Lemma
5.14
, there are three possibilities for the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{H})
: it can be the whole
\mathsf{G}_{-}
, or it can be contained in a maximal proper reductive subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
, or else it can be contained in a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
. Since the projection action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{-}
is transitive, the second option can once again be ruled out. Suppose that
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathsf{G}_{-}}(\mathsf{H})
is contained in some maximal proper parabolic subgroup
\mathsf{Q}
of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
. After conjugating by an element of
\mathsf{G}_{-}
, we may assume that
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{Q}_{j}
is the parabolic subgroup associated with the subset of simple roots
\Phi_{j}=\Lambda\setminus\{\alpha_{j}\}
for some
\alpha_{j}\in\Lambda
. Thanks to Theorem
5.7
and Remark
5.5
,
\mathsf{H}
then has the same orbits as the subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{j}^{\Lambda}
obtained by canonical extension of some cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}_{j}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{j}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{j}
is a closed connected subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(B_{j})
. According to Lemma
5.6
, the action of
\mathsf{H}_{j}
on
M_{0}\times B_{j}
does not decompose—and it still has a singular orbit. In case the projection of
\mathsf{H}_{j}
in
I^{0}(B_{j})
is not the whole
I^{0}(B_{j})
and is thus contained in a maximal proper parabolic subgroup of
I^{0}(B_{j})
, we can run the same argument again. After several iterations, we are bound to arrive at the following situation: the action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
is orbit-equivalent to the canonical extension of a cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
, where:

(a)

\Phi\subseteq\Lambda
is some subset of simple roots,

(b)

\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
is a closed connected subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
,

(c)

\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times B_{\Phi}
does not decompose.

(d)

The projection of
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
to
I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
is surjective.

Note that
\Phi
cannot be empty, for otherwise the canonical extension
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}^{\Lambda}\curvearrowright M_{0}\times M_{-}
would decompose. Since
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
is a proper connected Lie subgroup of
(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
, it must be contained in a maximal proper connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{E}\subsetneq(\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
. The projection actions of
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
on
M_{0}
and
B_{\Phi}
are transitive, so the same is true for
\mathsf{E}
. Moreover, the projection of
\mathsf{E}
to
I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
has to be surjective in view of item (d) above. According to Lemma
5.14
, we must then have
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{1}\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
for some maximal proper connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{E}_{1}
of
\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose action on
M_{0}
is transitive. Notice that the action of
\mathsf{E}_{1}\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
on
M_{0}\times M_{-}
is still transitive, so
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}
must be contained in some maximal proper connected Lie subgroup
\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{E}_{1}\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
. Arguing in the same vein, we see that
\mathsf{E}^{\prime}=\mathsf{E}_{2}\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
, where
\mathsf{E}_{2}\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{1}
is a maximal proper connected Lie subgroup that acts on
M_{0}
transitively. By iterating this process, we can construct a chain of maximal proper connected Lie subgroups

\dots\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i+1}\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{1}\subsetneq\mathsf{SO}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that
\mathsf{E}_{i}
acts on
M_{0}
transitively and
\mathsf{H}_{\Phi}\subsetneq\mathsf{E}_{i}\times I^{0}(B_{\Phi})
for all
i
. On the one hand, by dimension reasons, we can only have a finite number of such iterations. On the other hand, as explained above, we should always be able to make one more step. This yields a contradiction and completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

As explained in the introduction, when combined together, Theorems
B
,
C
, and
D
imply Theorem
A
.

6.
The non-simply connected case

In this section, we look at cohomogeneity-one actions on non-simply connected symmetric spaces. If
M
is such a space, its universal cover
\widetilde{M}
is also symmetric. Theorem
A
asserts that every cohomogeneity-one action on
\widetilde{M}=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
either decomposes or else is orbit-equivalent to the action of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
. In each case, we study how the action behaves with respect to the covering
\pi\colon\widetilde{M}\to M
, whether it descends to a proper cohomogeneity-one action on
M
, and what happens to orbit equivalence. The section culminates with Theorem
6.4
, which gives a description of all cohomogeneity-one actions on
M
via those on
\widetilde{M}
and can be regarded as the extension of Theorem
A
to the non-simply connected case.

Let
M
be a connected Riemannian manifold and
\pi\colon\widetilde{M}\rightarrow M
its universal Riemannian covering. We would like to investigate how (orbit equivalence classes of) isometric actions on
M
are related to those on
\widetilde{M}
. Write
\Aut(\pi)
for the group of deck transformations of
\pi
. This is a discrete subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M})
that acts on
\widetilde{M}
freely and properly discontinuously and acts simply transitively on each fiber of
\pi
. Moreover, each of its elements is fully determined by its value at any single point of
\widetilde{M}
. We also have
\Aut(\pi)\cong\pi_{1}(M)
. We say that an isometry
\widetilde{f}\in I(\widetilde{M})

descends
to
M
if it permutes the fibers of
\pi
—and thus uniquely determines a
descent

f\in I(M)
such that
\pi\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ\pi
; we will also say that
\widetilde{f}
is a
lift
of
f
. We write
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
for the subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M})
consisting of elements that descend to
M
. It is straightforward to check that
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
coincides with the normalizer of
\Aut(\pi)
in
I(\widetilde{M})
and is thus a closed subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M})
. By design, we have a homomorphism
\Pi\colon I(\widetilde{M},\pi)\to I(M)
sending
\widetilde{f}
to
f
, and its kernel is precisely
\Aut(\pi)
. By the lifting criterion, any isometry of
M
admits lifts to
\widetilde{M}
, so
\Pi
is a surjective local isomorphism.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
isometrically. By quotienting out the ineffectiveness kernel, we may assume that
\mathsf{H}
is a Lie subgroup of
I^{0}(M)
. We define the
lift
of
\mathsf{H}
(resp., of
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
) to be the subgroup
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}:=\Pi^{-1}(\mathsf{H})^{0}\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
(resp., the action
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\curvearrowright\widetilde{M}
). Conversely, suppose
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
is a connected Lie group acting on
\widetilde{M}
isometrically such that its image in
I(\widetilde{M)}
is contained in
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
(without loss of generality, we assume
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
). Note that
\mathsf{H}:=\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}})
is a connected Lie subgroup of
I(M)
of the same dimensions as
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
