. As
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
is a connected Lie subgroup of
\Pi^{-1}(\mathsf{H})
, it must be its identity component, which is exactly the lift of
\mathsf{H}
. We call
\mathsf{H}
(resp.,
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
) the
descent
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
(resp., of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\curvearrowright\widetilde{M}
). We see that a connected Lie subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M})
is a lift if and only if it is contained in
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
(we will also say that its action on
\widetilde{M}

descends
to
M
in this case).

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie subgroup of
I(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
its lift. Notice that
\Pi
restricts to a surjective local isomorphism from
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
to
\mathsf{H}
. For any
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}
, we have
\pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\widetilde{p})=\mathsf{H}\cdot p
, where
p=\pi(\widetilde{p})
, so
\pi
maps the orbits of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
in
\widetilde{M}
onto the orbits of
\mathsf{H}
in
M
. Moreover, one readily sees that the isotropy subgroup
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\widetilde{p}}
is an open subgroup of
\Pi^{-1}(\mathsf{H}_{p})\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
, so

\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}/\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\widetilde{p}}\cong\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\widetilde{p}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.7361pt\pi\hskip 5.7361pt}\mathsf{H}\cdot p\cong\mathsf{H}/\mathsf{H}_{p}\cong\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}/(\Pi^{-1}(\mathsf{H}_{p})\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{H}})

is a covering map. We see that the lift procedure preserves the cohomogeneity of an action and induces a surjective mapping between the corresponding orbit spaces:
\widetilde{M}/\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\to M/\mathsf{H}
.

The following proposition outlines some further properties of the lift procedure and gives a handy necessary criterion for an action on
\widetilde{M}
to be a lift. All actions in the proposition are assumed to be by connected Lie groups by default.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
M
be a connected Riemannian manifold and
\pi\colon\widetilde{M}\rightarrow M
its universal Riemannian covering.

(a)

If two isometric actions on
M
are orbit-equivalent, then so are their lifts. Conversely, if two isometric actions on
\widetilde{M}
descend to
M
and are orbit-equivalent via an element of
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, then their descents are orbit-equivalent as well.

(b)

If an isometric action in
\widetilde{M}
descends to
M
, then
\Aut(\pi)\cap I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
permutes its orbits.

(c)

The lift of a proper isometric action on
M
is proper. Conversely, if
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, then its descent determines a proper action on
M
if and only if there exists a neighborhood
U
of the identity in
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
(or
I(\widetilde{M})
) such that for every
g\in\Aut(\pi)
, either
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
or
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU=\varnothing
.

Remark 6.2
.

It might happen that two isometric actions on
\widetilde{M}
are orbit-equivalent and one of them descends to
M
but the other does not.

Proof.

We start with (a). Suppose two actions
\mathsf{H}_{1}\curvearrowright M
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}\curvearrowright M
are orbit-equivalent via
g\in I(M)
, i.e.,
g(\mathsf{H}_{1}\cdot p)=\mathsf{H}_{2}\cdot g(p)
for all
p\in M
. Take any lift
\widetilde{g}
of
g
and fix
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}
lying over
p\in M
. We would like to show that
\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{1}\cdot\widetilde{p})=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{2}\cdot\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{p})
. We observe:

\pi(\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{1}\cdot\widetilde{p}))=g(\pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{1}\cdot\widetilde{p}))=g(\mathsf{H}_{1}\cdot p)=\mathsf{H}_{2}\cdot g(p)=\pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{2}\cdot\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{p})),

(7)

which means that the restrictions of
\pi
to both submanifolds in question are covering maps over
g(\mathsf{H}_{1}\cdot p)=\mathsf{H}_{2}\cdot g(p)
. Since the two submanifolds share a common point
\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{p})
, the uniqueness property of lifting paths ensures that they coincide. Conversely, if we have two connected Lie subgroups
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{2}\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
and their actions on
\widetilde{M}
are orbit-equivalent via
\widetilde{g}\in I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, a calculation similar to (
7
) shows that their descents are orbit-equivalent via
\Pi(\widetilde{g})
.

For (b), first note that
\Aut(\pi)\cap I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
is the kernel of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)}}
, so it is a discrete normal subgroup of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. Since the latter is connected,
\Aut(\pi)\cap I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
is actually a central subgroup of it. If
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
is a connected Lie subgroup and
\widetilde{g}\in\Aut(\pi)\cap I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, we have
\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\widetilde{p})=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\widetilde{g}(\widetilde{p})
for any
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}
, which was to be shown.

Finally, we prove (c). If
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
is proper, then (the image of)
\mathsf{H}
is closed in
I(M)
and hence so is its lift
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
in
I(\widetilde{M})
. This means that the action of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}
is proper. Let now
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
be a closed connected subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. First of all, whether the neighborhood
U
in (c) is chosen in
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
or
I(\widetilde{M})
is irrelevant because
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
is closed in
I(\widetilde{M})
. Second, notice that as soon as
U
is a connected neighborhood of the identity in
I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, the intersection
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU
is empty for any
g\in\Aut(\pi)\setminus I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. The statement then follows from a more general Lie-theoretical fact that we prove separately in Lemma
6.3
below.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\Pi\colon\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}\to\mathsf{G}
be a local isomorphism between connected Lie groups with kernel
\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
be a closed subgroup. Then the image
\mathsf{H}=\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}})
is a closed subgroup of
\mathsf{G}
if and only if the following condition is satisfied: there exists a neighborhood
U
of the identity in
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
such that for every
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}
, either
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
or
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Notice that
\Pi
is a covering map and
\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}
is a discrete cental subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
that can be identified with the group of deck transformations. First, assume that the aforementioned condition is satisfied. We would like to prove that
\mathsf{H}
is closed in
\mathsf{G}
. It suffices to show that it is locally closed (see
[
Bou98
, Ch. III, §2, Prop. 4]
), i.e., that there exists a neighborhood
V
of the identity in
\mathsf{G}
such that
\mathsf{H}\cap V
is closed in
V
. Let
U
be a neighborhood of the identity in
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
as in the statement of the lemma. By shrinking it if necessary, we may assume that
\Pi
restricts to a diffeomorphism between
U
and
V=\Pi(U)
and that the latter is evenly covered. The connected components of
\Pi^{-1}(V)
are given by
gU
as
g
runs through
\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}
. If
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
, then the restriction
L_{g}\colon U\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.3941pt\hskip-6.3941pt\hbox{$\xrightarrow{\phantom{\sim}}$}\hskip-6.3941pt\hskip-4.76384pt\raisebox{0.64444pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\sim\,$}}\hskip-4.76384pt\hskip 6.3941pt}}gU
sends
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U
diffeomorphically onto
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU
. Since
g
is a deck transformation, we see that
\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U)=\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)
inside
V
. We then compute:

\mathsf{H}\cap V=\;\bigcup_{g\hskip 1.0pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}}\,\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)\,=\;\Big(\hskip 6.00006pt\bigcup_{g\hskip 1.0pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\hskip 1.0pt\cap\hskip 1.0pt\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}\,\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)\Big)\;\cup\;\Big(\hskip 6.00006pt\bigcup_{g\hskip 1.0pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\setminus\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}\,\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)\Big)\,=\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U).

Here the sets in the first union all coincide, whereas the second union is empty by assumption. Since
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U
is closed in
U
, its image under
\Pi
is closed in
V
, which was to be shown.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathsf{H}
is closed and thus embedded. In that case, we can find an evenly covered neighborhood
V
of the identity in
\mathsf{G}
whose intersection with
\mathsf{H}
is connected. Let
U
be the connected component of
\Pi^{-1}(V)
containing the identity; the other connected components are then given by
gU
as
g
runs through
\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}
. As we argued above, if
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
, then the image of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU
under
\Pi
coincides with that of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U
. That image is an open subset of
\mathsf{H}\cap V
. Suppose now that
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\setminus\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
. Observe that
g(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U)
has to be disjoint from
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU
. Since
g
is a deck transformation, this is equivalent to saying that
\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U)
has to be disjoint from
\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)
. Note that the latter is also an open subset of
\mathsf{H}\cap V
. We can thus form an open subset
\bigcup_{g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\setminus\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)
of
\mathsf{H}\cap V
. It is still disjoint from
\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap U)=\bigcup_{g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap gU)
, yet its union with the latter has to be the whole
\mathsf{H}\cap V
. By connectedness of
\mathsf{H}\cap V
, we deduce that
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
intersects
gU
trivially whenever
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}\setminus\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The lifting procedure allows one to shift the study of isometric actions on a Riemannian manifold to that on its universal cover. In order to properly understand which actions on
\widetilde{M}
descend to
M
, one has to understand what the group of deck transformations looks like and how it is embedded into
I(\widetilde{M})
. In general, this can be complicated, but for symmetric spaces the picture is rather simple.

Suppose that
M
is a symmetric space, and let
\widetilde{M}=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
be the decomposition of its universal cover into its compact, Euclidean, and noncompact factors. Since
M
is symmetric,
\Aut(\pi)\cong\pi_{1}(M)
is abelian. In fact,
[
Wol11
, Th. 8.3.12]
provides a complete description of
\Aut(\pi)
as a subgroup of
I(\widetilde{M})
. Let us write
\Delta
for the centralizer of
I^{0}(M_{+})
in
I(M_{+})
, which is a finite abelian subgroup. As before, we identify
M_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
and thus have
I(M_{0})=\mathsf{O}_{n}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then,
\Aut(\pi)
is a discrete subgroup
5
5

5

Conversely, for every discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\widetilde{M}/\Gamma
is symmetric.
of
\Delta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This implies that the projection of
\Aut(\pi)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a lattice (possibly not of full rank), which we denote by
L
.
We write
V=\mathrm{span}(L)\simeq\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
V^{\perp}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\ominus V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
. We also denote by
L^{*}\subseteq V
the dual lattice of
L
, i.e.,
L^{*}=\{v\in V\mid\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{w}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt\in\mathbb{Z}\;\,\text{for every}\;w\in L\}
. Since
\Delta
is finite, the intersection
L^{\prime}=\Aut(\pi)\cap L
is a sub-lattice of finite index in
L
. Moreover,
\Aut(\pi)\subseteq\Delta\times L\subseteq\Delta\times V
, and we have

M\cong\widetilde{M}/\Aut(\pi)\cong M_{\mathrm{comp}}\times V^{\perp}\times M_{-},

(8)

where
M_{\mathrm{comp}}=(M_{+}\times V)/\Aut(\pi)
is a compact symmetric space that is finitely covered by
M_{+}\times V/L^{\prime}\simeq M_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{k}
. We can also easily obtain an explicit description of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. Indeed, since
\Aut(\pi)\subseteq I(M_{+})\times I(M_{0})
, we have
I(M_{-})\subseteq I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. By definition, every element of
I^{0}(M_{+})
centralizes
\Delta
. Similarly, every translation in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centralizes
L
. This implies that
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)=I^{0}(M_{+})\times(\mathsf{N}^{0}_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}(\Aut(\pi))\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
. Now, every element
g
of
\mathsf{N}^{0}_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}(\Aut(\pi))
must normalize
L
. Since
\mathsf{N}^{0}_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}(\Aut(\pi))
is connected,
g
must centralize
L
and is thus the identity on
V
. We deduce that

I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)=I^{0}(M_{+})\times(\mathsf{SO}(V^{\perp})\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{n})\times I^{0}(M_{-}).

(9)

With respect to decompositions (
8
) and (
9
), it is not difficult to see that
\Pi
sends
I^{0}(M_{+})\times V
onto
I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})
,
\mathsf{SO}(V^{\perp})\ltimes V^{\perp}=I^{0}(V^{\perp})
onto itself, and
I^{0}(M_{-})
onto itself. This shows a posteriori that
I^{0}(M)=I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})\times I^{0}(V^{\perp})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
.

We are now ready to fully reduce the classification problem for cohomogeneity-one actions on non-simply connected symmetric spaces to that on simply connected ones. It will prove useful to introduce the following bit of notation: given
\xi\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
, we will write
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
for the connected Lie subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
with Lie algebra
((\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a})\ominus\xi)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\displaystyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[9.99997pt]{\raisebox{-0.83333pt}{\begin{picture}\textstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83333pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[8.36108pt]{\raisebox{-0.83334pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}{\makebox[7.36115pt]{\raisebox{-0.83336pt}{\begin{picture}\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3
\roundcap\put(0.5,0.5){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\raisebox{0.83336pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}
\put(0.5,0.5){\arc[270,90]{0.5}}
\end{picture}}}}}\mathfrak{n}
. This corresponds to taking
\mathfrak{v}=\mathbb{R}\xi
in Remark
1.4
and thus generalizes the subgroups
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
(with
\xi=||E||^{2}X-||X||^{2}E
),
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
(for
\xi\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}
), and
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{X}
(for
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}
). Here and below, it will be convenient for us to identify
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{A}
by means of the exponential map. In particular, we can think of the normal vector
\xi
as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{A}
, which allows us to decompose it as
\xi=E_{V}+E_{V^{\perp}}+X
, where
E_{V}\in V
,
E_{V^{\perp}}\in V^{\perp}
, and
X\in\mathsf{A}\simeq\mathfrak{a}
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
M
be a symmetric space and
\widetilde{M}=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
its universal cover. Write
M=M_{\mathrm{comp}}\times V^{\perp}\times M_{-}
as above. Let
\mathsf{H}
be a connected Lie group acting on
M
properly, isometrically, and with cohomogeneity one. Then the action of
\mathsf{H}
is orbit-equivalent to one of the following:

(a)

The action of
\Pi(\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I^{0}(M_{-}))
, where
\mathsf{H}_{+}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{+})
acting on
M_{+}
with cohomogeneity one.

(b)

The action of
I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})\times\mathsf{H}_{V^{\perp}}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
, where
\mathsf{H}_{V^{\perp}}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(V^{\perp})
acting on
V^{\perp}
with cohomogeneity one.

(c)

The action of
I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})\times V^{\perp}\times\mathsf{H}_{-}
, where
\mathsf{H}_{-}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{-})
acting on
M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one.

(d)

The action of
\Pi(I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi})
with
\xi=E_{V}+E_{V^{\perp}}+X\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
, where either
E_{V}\neq 0
or
E\neq 0\neq X
, and there exists a nonzero
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
cE_{V}\in L^{*}
.

Conversely, each of the above actions is proper and isometric and has cohomogeneity one.

Remark 6.5
.

A few comments are in order. First of all, implicit in the theorem is the fact that the actions in (a) and (d) are well-defined, i.e., that the corresponding descents actually exist. Notice that, by design, the actions in (b) and (c) decompose with respect to the decomposition
M=M_{\mathrm{comp}}\times V^{\perp}\times M_{-}
. In fact, even though it is written as a descent, the action in (a) also decomposes. To see this, note that
\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
can be written as
(\mathsf{H}_{+}\times V)\times V^{\perp}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
. The descent of this subgroup is
\Pi(\mathsf{H}_{+}\times V)\times V^{\perp}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
, where
\Pi(\mathsf{H}_{+}\times V)\subseteq I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})
. On the contrary, as can be traced in the proof below, the actions in (d) do not decompose. The assumption that either
E_{V}\neq 0
or
E\neq 0\neq X
is only added in (d) to avoid intersections with (b) and (c): if
E_{V}=X=0
, then
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}=V\times(V^{\perp}\ominus E_{V^{\perp}})\times(A\ltimes N)
, leading to (b). Similarly, for
E=0\neq X
,
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times((A\ominus X)\ltimes N)
, which leads to (c). Finally, the other assumption on
E_{V}
in (d), despite looking elaborate, is only there to ensure that the descended action is proper. It means that the line spanned by
E_{V}
passes through (nonzero elements of) the dual lattice
L^{*}
(unless
E_{V}=0
, in which case the assumption is automatically satisfied). Loosely speaking, it says that
E_{V}
is only allowed to run in directions that are “rational” with respect to
L
.

We begin by dealing with arguably the most technical part of the proof: checking when the descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
is proper. Note that this is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
acting on
\widetilde{M}
with cohomogeneity one. In view of (
9
), it is contained in
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
and thus descends to
M
. In Proposition
6.1
(c), we worked out a general criterion for the descent of a proper action to be proper. We now refine that criterion in the special case of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
, making it more user-friendly.

Lemma 6.6
.

Given any
\xi=E_{V}+E_{V^{\perp}}+X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{A}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a nonzero
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
cE_{V}\in L^{*}
.

(ii)

The descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
acts on
M
properly.

(iii)

The descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
acts on
M
with closed orbits.

Proof.

According to Proposition
6.1
(c), the descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
acts properly if and only if there exists a neighborhood
U
of the identity in
I(\widetilde{M})
such that for every
g\in\Aut(\pi)
, either
g\in I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
or
(I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi})\cap gU=\varnothing
. We now show that this condition is equivalent to (i). First of all, recall that the multiplication map

I^{0}(M_{+})\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{SO}_{n})\times(\mathsf{K}_{-}\times\mathsf{A}\times\mathsf{N})\rightarrow I^{0}(M_{+})\times I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})\simeq I^{0}(\widetilde{M})

(10)

is a diffeomorphism. In particular, for every element
g=(g_{+},g_{0},g_{-})\in I^{0}(\widetilde{M})
, we have unique decompositions
g_{0}=v\circ T
with
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
T\in\mathsf{SO}_{n}
and
g_{-}=g_{\mathsf{K}_{-}}g_{\mathsf{A}}g_{\mathsf{N}}
with
g_{\mathsf{K}_{-}}\in\mathsf{K}_{-},\,g_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{A},
and
g_{\mathsf{N}}\in\mathsf{N}
. This decomposition naturally depends on the order in which we multiply the factors. From now on, we fix the order as in (
10
), since this will simplify our formulas.

Let
U
be a neighborhood of the identity in
I(\widetilde{M})
. By shrinking it if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
U
is connected and splits with respect to (
10
) as

U=U_{+}\times U_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\times U_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}\times U_{\mathsf{K}_{-}}\times U_{\mathsf{A}}\times U_{\mathsf{N}}.

Given any
g=(g_{+},v,e)\in\Aut(\pi)\subseteq\Delta\times L\subseteq I^{0}(M_{+})\times I^{0}(M_{0})\times I^{0}(M_{-})
, we have

gU=g_{+}U_{+}\times(v+U_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\times U_{\mathsf{SO}_{n}}\times U_{\mathsf{K}_{-}}\times U_{\mathsf{A}}\times U_{\mathsf{N}}.

(11)

Note that an element
h=(h_{+},h_{0},h_{-})
of
I(\widetilde{M})
lies in
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
if and only if
h_{+}\in I^{0}(M_{+}),\,h_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,h_{-}=h_{\mathsf{A}}h_{\mathsf{N}}\in\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
, and
(h_{0},h_{\mathsf{A}})\perp\xi
, where the inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times A
is simply carried over from
\mathfrak{r}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
. Therefore, the set
gU
intersects
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
nontrivially if and only if
g_{+}\in I^{0}(M_{+})
and
(v+U_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\times U_{\mathsf{A}}
contains elements orthogonal to
\xi
. For small
U
, the latter just means that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is sufficiently small. Therefore, the condition in Proposition
6.1
(c) reads here as follows: there exists
\delta>0
such that for every
(g_{+},v)\in\Aut(\pi)
with
g_{+}\in I^{0}(M_{+})
, either
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
(that is,
v\in\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
) or
|\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt|\geq\delta
. The set of such vectors
v
forms a sub-lattice of
L
, which we temporarily denote by
L^{\prime\prime}
. Note that
L^{\prime\prime}
clearly contains
L^{\prime}=\Aut(\pi)\cap L
and is thus a subgroup of
L
of finite index.
Since
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
, the above condition simply becomes the question of how
E_{V}
is situated in
V
with respect to the lattice
L^{\prime\prime}
. The condition that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{v}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is either zero or separated from zero by at least
\delta
for every
v\in L^{\prime\prime}
is equivalent to asking that the image of
L^{\prime\prime}
under the functional
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, the latter is equivalent to asking that the image of the whole
L
under
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is discrete in
\mathbb{R}
.
If there exists
c\neq 0
such that
cE_{V}\in L^{*}
(that is,
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{-}\hskip 0.5pt,{cE_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
only takes integral values on
L
), then this is of course the case. Conversely, if
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{L}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
, then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
of
L
such that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{e_{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
. If
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{e_{k}}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=0
, any
c
works; otherwise, we take
c=\frac{1}{\langle\hskip 1.0pt{e_{k}}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.16391pt}
. Altogether, we conclude that (i) is equivalent to (ii).

Since (ii) readily implies (iii), to finish the proof of the lemma, we are left to show that (iii) implies (ii). Arguing by contradiction, suppose that the descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}\curvearrowright\widetilde{M}
is not proper. Since the orbits of the descent are the images of the orbits of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
in
\widetilde{M}
under
\pi
, we need to show that those images are nonclosed. We can regard the covering
\pi
as a composition

\widetilde{M}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt\pi^{\prime}\,}\widetilde{M}/L^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt\pi^{\prime\prime}\,}(\widetilde{M}/L^{\prime})/(\Aut(\pi)/L^{\prime})=\widetilde{M}/\Aut(\pi)=M.

We first prove that the images of the orbits of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
under
\pi^{\prime}
are nonclosed in
\widetilde{M}/L^{\prime}
. Notice that
\pi^{\prime}
only affects
M_{0}
:

\pi^{\prime}\colon M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}\to M_{+}\times M_{0}/L^{\prime}\times M_{-}.

Every orbit of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
in
\widetilde{M}
is the product of
M_{+}
with an orbit of
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
in
M_{0}\times M_{-}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that the images of the orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
under
M_{0}\times M_{-}\to M_{0}/L^{\prime}\times M_{-}
are nonclosed. If we replace
M_{0}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
M_{-}
with its solvable model
\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}
, the latter map becomes a local isomorphism of Lie groups:

\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}/L^{\prime}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N}).

The orbits of
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
are simply its right cosets, and the images of those orbits under
\Psi
are the right cosets of
\Psi(\mathsf{H}_{\xi})
. So we need to show that
\Psi(\mathsf{H}_{\xi})
is a nonclosed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n}/L^{\prime}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
. By assumption, condition (ii) (and thus condition (i)) fails to hold. As we showed earlier, this is equivalent to saying that
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{L}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt=\langle\hskip 1.0pt{L}\hskip 0.5pt,{E_{V}}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
is a nondiscrete subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. Since
L^{\prime}
has finite index in
L
, this is in turn equivalent to
\langle\hskip 1.0pt{L^{\prime}}\hskip 0.5pt,{\xi}\hskip 1.0pt\rangle\hskip 0.20004pt
being nondiscrete in
\mathbb{R}
. But that means that for every neighborhood
U
of the identity in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
, there exists
g\in L^{\prime}\setminus\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
such that
g\hskip 1.0ptU
intersects
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
nontrivially. Lemma
6.3
then implies that the image
\Psi(\mathsf{H}_{\xi})
is a nonclosed Lie subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n}/L^{\prime}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
, which was to be shown.

Now, let
S\subseteq\widetilde{M}
by any orbit of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
. We can write
S=M_{+}\times C
, where
C\subseteq M_{0}\times M_{-}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathsf{A}\ltimes\mathsf{N})
is a right coset of
\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
. We know that
\pi^{\prime}(S)=M_{+}\times\Phi(C)
is nonclosed in
M_{+}\times M_{0}/L^{\prime}\times M_{-}
, and we are left to show that its image under
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is nonclosed in
M
. Since
\Phi(C)
is nonclosed in
M_{0}/L^{\prime}\times M_{-}
, its closure is a submanifold of strictly larger dimension, and the same is thus true about the closure of
\pi^{\prime}(S)
. Note that
\pi^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(S))
is closed if and only if
\pi^{\prime\prime-1}(\pi^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(S)))=\bigcup_{g\in\Aut(\pi)/L^{\prime}}g\pi^{\prime}(S)
is closed. As the group
\Aut(\pi)/L^{\prime}
is finite, we have

\overline{\pi^{\prime\prime-1}(\pi^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(S)))}\;=\;\overline{\bigcup_{g\in\Aut(\pi)/L^{\prime}}g\pi^{\prime}(S)}\;=\bigcup_{g\in\Aut(\pi)/L^{\prime}}g\hskip 1.0pt\overline{\pi^{\prime}(S)},

and the latter set is the union of submanifolds of strictly larger dimension. This implies that
\pi^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(S))=\pi(S)
is nonclosed, which completes the proof.
∎

With the lemma out of the way, we can now proceed to the proof of the theorem.

Proof of Theorem
6.4
.

We begin by checking that the actions listed in (a)-(d) are indeed proper, isometric, and of cohomogeneity one. For (b) and (c), this is trivial. It follows from our discussion earlier in the section that if
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
is a closed connected subgroup acting on
\widetilde{M}
with cohomogeneity one and satisfying the condition in Proposition
6.1
(c), then it descends to a proper isometric cohomogeneity-one action
\Pi(\widetilde{\mathsf{H}})\curvearrowright M
. We use this approach with the actions in (a) and (d), as those are expressed as descents. For every subgroup
\mathsf{H}_{+}\subseteq I^{0}(M_{+})
as in (a),
\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
thanks to (
9
), and it clearly acts on
\widetilde{M}
with cohomogeneity one. Thus, we need only verify that it satisfies the criterion from Proposition
6.1
(c). Take a neighborhood
U_{+}
of the identity in
I(M_{+})
such that
gU_{+}\cap\mathsf{H}_{+}=\varnothing
for every
g\in\Delta\setminus\mathsf{H}_{+}
(which exists because
\Delta
is finite and
\mathsf{H}_{+}
is closed). Then
U=U_{+}\times I(M_{0})\times I(M_{-})
is a neighborhood of the identity in
I(\widetilde{M})
that satisfies the condition. Similarly, for every
\xi
as in (d),
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
acting on
\widetilde{M}
with cohomogeneity one. It then descends to
M
, and its descent acts properly thanks to Lemma
6.6
.

Now we prove that any proper isometric cohomogeneity-one action
\mathsf{H}\curvearrowright M
with
\mathsf{H}
connected is orbit-equivalent to one of the actions in (a)-(d). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathsf{H}\subseteq I^{0}(M)
and write
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
for its lift. It follows from our discussion earlier in the section that
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
acting on
\widetilde{M}
with cohomogeneity one, and its descent is
\mathsf{H}
. According to Theorem
A
, the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}=M_{+}\times M_{0}\times M_{-}
either decomposes or is orbit-equivalent to the action of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
for some nonzero
E\in\mathfrak{r}^{n}
and
X\in\mathfrak{a}
. We consider these cases separately.

To begin with, suppose that the projection action
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}\curvearrowright M_{+}
is of cohomogeneity one. In this case, the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}
has the same orbits as
\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I^{0}(M_{-})\subseteq I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, where
\mathsf{H}_{+}
is some closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{+})
acting on
M_{+}
with cohomogeneity one. In view of Proposition
6.1
(a), the descent of
\mathsf{H}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I^{0}(M_{-})\curvearrowright\widetilde{M}
has the same orbits as the action of
\mathsf{H}
on
M
. This corresponds to item (a) on the list.

Similarly, if the projection action
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}\curvearrowright M_{-}
has cohomogeneity one, then the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}
has the same orbits as
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{-}\subseteq I(\widetilde{M},\pi)
, where
\mathsf{H}_{-}
is some closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{-})
acting on
M_{-}
with cohomogeneity one. In the same vein, the descent of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathsf{H}_{-}
has the same orbits in
M
as
\mathsf{H}
. Note that, with respect to the decomposition
M=M_{\mathrm{comp}}\times V^{\perp}\times M_{-}
, this descent also has the same orbits as
I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})\times V^{\perp}\times\mathsf{H}_{-}
. This corresponds to item (c).

Going further, suppose that the projection action
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}\curvearrowright M_{0}
is of cohomogeneity one. In this case, the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}
has the same orbits as
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{0}\times I^{0}(M_{-})
, where
\mathsf{H}_{0}
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(M_{0})
acting on
M_{0}
with cohomogeneity one. Owing to the classification of cohomogeneity-one actions on Euclidean spaces, we know that the action of
\mathsf{H}_{0}
is orbit-equivalent via an element of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the action of the group
\mathsf{SO}(W^{\perp})\times W
, where
W\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is some proper subspace. If
\dim(W)=n-1
, the orbits are affine hyperplanes parallel to
W
. Otherwise, they are generalized cylinders around
W
(which includes spheres when
W=\{0\}
). First, assume that
V\subseteq W
. In this case, we see that the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
is orbit-equivalent to that of

I^{0}(M_{+})\times V\times\big(\mathsf{SO}(W^{\perp})\times(W\ominus V)\big)\times I^{0}(M_{-}).

(12)

Here
\mathsf{SO}(W^{\perp})\times(W\ominus V)
is a closed connected subgroup of
I^{0}(V^{\perp})
acting on
V^{\perp}
with cohomogeneity one. In view of (
9
) and the subsequent paragraph, the subgroup (
12
) descends to
I^{0}(M_{\mathrm{comp}})\times\left(\mathsf{SO}(W^{\perp})\times(W\ominus V)\right)\times I^{0}(M_{-})
, whose action on
M
is orbit-equivalent to that of
\mathsf{H}
thanks to Proposition
6.1
(a). This corresponds to item (b) on the list. We claim that the assumption
V\subseteq W
automatically holds true whenever
\dim(W)<n-1
. Indeed, according to Proposition
6.1
(b), the orbits of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
(and thus of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times(\mathsf{SO}(W^{\perp})\times W)\times I^{0}(M_{-})
) must get permuted by the action of
L^{\prime}\subseteq\Aut(\pi)\cap I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. Since
L^{\prime}
spans
V
, this easily implies that
V
must be contained in
W
.

We are thus only left to consider the case when
\dim(W)=n-1
and
V\not\subseteq W
. Under these assumptions, we can write
W=\mathbb{R}^{n}\ominus E
for some
E
with
E_{V}\neq 0
. We see that
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
has the same orbits in
\widetilde{M}
as
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E}
. We already know that the latter subgroup descends to
M
and thus has the same orbits as
\mathsf{H}
thanks to Proposition
6.1
(a). Since those orbits are closed, Lemma
6.6
implies that there exists a nonzero
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
cE_{V}\in L^{*}
. This corresponds to item (d) with
\xi=E
.

Finally, we come to the case when the action of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
on
\widetilde{M}
is orbit-equivalent to that of
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
for some nonzero
E
and
X
. Note that the latter is a subgroup of
I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
and thus it descends to
M
. As follows from our discussion in Section 4 and in particular in Lemma
4.4
, an orbit equivalence between the actions of
\mathsf{\widetilde{H}}
and
I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X}
can actually be achieved by an element of
I^{0}(M_{-})\subseteq I^{0}(\widetilde{M},\pi)
. Proposition
6.1
(a) then implies that the action of
\Pi(I^{0}(M_{+})\times\mathsf{H}_{E,X})
on
M
is orbit-equivalent to that of
\mathsf{H}
. Now, as we mentioned earlier,
\mathsf{H}_{E,X}=\mathsf{H}_{\xi}
, where
\xi=E^{\prime}+X^{\prime}
with
E^{\prime}=-||X||^{2}E
and
X^{\prime}=||E||^{2}X
. As the orbits of the descent are closed, Lemma
6.6
implies that there exists a nonzero
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
cE^{\prime}_{V}\in L^{*}
. This also corresponds to item (d).
∎
